PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS. -- A Grim always thinks a man's heart is in the right place when she possesses it. WriEN a man becomes great, his friends re- member many things about him that never hap- pened. Lulu?. SISTER: "What's the diff'rence 'tween 'lectricity and lightnin' ?" Little Brother "You don't have to pay nuthin' fur lightnin'." HARRY—" Has Mabel's engagement been an- nounced yet?" Ethel—" No, but she blushes furiously every time is name his mentioned and says she just hates him." APLUESERTY—" Oi'd bike to take a trip around the wurruld," Mrs. M'Lubberty—" Sure, thot wud be foine 1" M'Lubherty—" Yis ; but t'nk av the cost av gittin' home ag'in !" "Tuts bread of yours, my dear, is a contradic- tion of the law f gravity." "Indeed?" Yes, it's as heavy as lead, but won't go down." "I HAVE frequently bought handsome pipes for my friends in my travels, but they never got them." "Go astray in shipping ? " "No; I keep them myself." NELL : "Wasn't it freakish of her to go rid- ing her wheel in evening dress?" Belle :"0 ! I don't know. I've heard of bare-back riders

before."

LEW : "Does Roguey know the police are on his track ?" Abe: "I helieve not. "The papers all say everything is being conducted with the utmost secrecy." CLARA : "I see Cynthi has decorated her room with guns, pistols, swords, and the like." Cora: " Yes • she always has been a great girl for having arms about her." ONE-HALF the people in this world are always trying to get the work to do that the other half is trying to get out of doing, and both are discon- tented because they can't succeed. INDISPENSAELE.—Pip : "Riches abused are a curse." Quip : "Yes ; but to see the true beauty of the proposition it is necessary to be one of the poor." .

I sent to bor- row their step-ladder, and they said they hadn't one, but if I'd wait a while they'd send and buy one." "There isn't much grazing there now, is there, grandpa?" was the comment of the youngest grandchild; and as the old gentleman rubbed his bare poll he had to admit the correct- ness of the assertion. NOT TO BE DONE.—A farmer was hiring reapers, but refused one poor man, who said: "Master, won't you hire me?" "No," said the farmer. " Why not?" Because you are too little." "Too little !" exclaimed Bam, " Does your honour reap the corn at the top ?" What could the Farmer do but laugh and send the little man to join his comrades in the field? "Bony Two or THEM."—The elder Dumas had had his times of struggle and knew what it was to be pressed for cash. In the days of his prosperity someone came to him and begged a loan of twenty- five francs. "It is to bury a friend," he ex- plained. "A friend?" said Dumas' "and what was he?" "He was a bailiff." The novelist went to his desk and took out a bill for fifty francs. "There," he said, placing this in the hands of the borrower. "here are fifty francs—bury two of them."

_ LOGICAL—The mention of whiskey and stoat recalls the amusing story once told by Father Mathew, the famous temperance advocate, of the inutility of using inconclusive arguments. He told some Dublin car drivers they might learn a lesson in temperance from the brute creation. "If," said he, "I were to set before one of your horses a bucket of water and a bucket of whiskey, you know which the wise beast would take." Whereupon one of the quick-witted carmen replied, "Well, Father, if I wor to place before me horse a truss of hay and, a beef steak, you know which the wise beast would chow. But does it follow that the hay is the best for me ?" RABBIT-PIE.—A gentleman, relating the inci- dents of his travels while in Paris, says :—I entered a restaurant and ordered a rabbit. I was green— verdant as the first cucumber—or I should not have done this. The rabbit came, and I offered a share to an old Frenchman opposite, whose eyes were fixed upon my plate; but he bowed a negative. "Monsieur has not been long in Paris?" he said. "No. I have just arrived," I answered. " Mon- sieur is going to eat that?" "Yes. May I offer you a slice?" "Monsieur will allow me to make a small observation?" inquired the Frenchman, with a grimace. "Certainly," I replied, becoming alarmed. "Monsieur, that rabbit once 'mewed,'' he replied. A COMMERCIAL traveller entered the hall of a hotel, and, picking up a pen, proceeded to make several perpendicular lines upon the register. When he had made half a dozen, more or less, he threw down the pen, with some exclamation about it being unfit to write with. Then he took up another and began making more straight marks, like capiial is with the shoulders cut off. By this time tile clerk was angry, and informed the stranger that the register was intended for signa- tures and must not be defaced in that manner. "Oh, don't get angry about it," said the man with the pen. He made a few horizontal dashes, and giving the register a twirl, showed the angry clerk that he had ody been writing his name, which was H. H. HILL. GOOD ADVICE.—A coloured man named Jim Webster was brought up before an American justice of the peace, and pleaded guilty after the evidence had been given. The judge, with a per. plexed look, said, "But I do not comprehend, Webster, how it was possible for you to steal those chickens when they were roosting right under the owner's window, and there were two vicious dogs in the yard." "Hit wouldn't do yer a bit of good, jedge, for me to splain how I cotched dem chickens, fer yer couldn't do it yerself if ye tried it forty times, and ye might get yer hide full of buckshot de berry. fast time yer put yer leg ober de fence. De bes' way fer yer ter do, jedge, is far yer ter bur ver chickens in de market."

The Most

'utritious.

Autumn fashions are in no hurry to makt, their appearance this season. This may arise from the fact that we have had an exceedingly bad summer for frills and furbelows, and have clung to the faint hope that sooner or later the time would surely come when we might don the dainty muslin and lace gowns bought earlier. Alas, by now we have resigned ourselves to the inevitable with as good a grace as possible and turn our attention to cloth and furs with the desire to find satisfaction in the evolution of smart toilettes. Modes are, however, as I said, not very, decided, for until October is well on its way nothing becomes definite- in the sartorial world. Still it is not difficult to define the ten- clency of things, and one of the most pronounced is that for out of door wear skirts will be of a more reasonable length, the long trailing and undoubtedly graceful dresses being kept for the house. This is certainly a step in the right direction, for it does seem the only sensible thing to have the skirt for street wear of a comfortable walking length, as Englishwomen are slow to acquire the trick of lifting their skirts gracefully. Indeed, as I recently saw it put, they clutch their dresses with both hands like professional weight lifters. There is a knack which our French sisters have eminently attained to of lifting their skirts in a neat and graceful manner so as to allow a little (but only just a little) of the dainty underskirt and neat ankle to be visible. The smart toilette sketched is of royal blue cloth made very simply with an

AN AUTUMN TOILETTN.

embroidered collar and waistband. The skirt is 3nt in one of the most fashionable styles for autumn which, although not exactly novel, is very popular and well suited to a walking .kirt. The skirt is arranged with a box-pleat introduced at the seams and widening out into greater fulness below the knees without being cumbersome. The coat is of the Russian blouse shape, a style very easy to turn out at home, as the back is devoid of any seam at all, and allowed to pouch over the band slightly. Of course, on a stout figure it is best to avoid all pouching and keep the back as flat as possible. The coat is fitted to the figure at the aide seam: and is completed by a basque. This is quite one 3f the features of autumn coats and is to bE seen in one or two styles. There is the basque which is cut away in front like a man's morning coat, and the pleated or godet basque which comes almost to the knees and gives something the effect of a double skirt. It requires, how- ever, a tall slight figure to carry off this style successfully when expressed in heavy materials. One smart basqued coat I saw recently had the basqe seams at the sides left open and laced half way down with silk cord, the cord being finished at both ends with tassels, for cordinga and tassels are very much worn. The smart hat illustrated is of ivory beaver trimmed with green wings and some black velvet ribbon, which is tied round the crown and finish in a bow at the back. The newest hats at present are of soft silky beaver in light pastel shades, and the combination of white or ivory and green is much in evidence. Some- times the stiff wings are replaced by sweeping Paradise plumes, but, of course, these are more expensive than the former, and cannot stand

the same amount of hard wear and exposure ta all sorts of weather. The small pork-pie hat with its stiff up-turned brim is also making a bid for favour, and is worn tipped forward over the nose, but it cannot be called generally becoming and seems to demand the presence of the immense chignon with which it was associated rather more than a quarter of a century ago. Bird's wings and plumage are very much used as a trimming, and it must be a comfort to anyone with sensitive feelings to know that they are for the most part manu- factured and made up from the feathers of the inmates of the barnyard, although, of course, in some few instances, the real wild bird is to be seen. Quills are as fashionable as ever, but are laid rather more flatly on the hat than before. The illustration given shows the latest fashion for arranging the veil, and the sketch was made from one of the best dressed woman of to-day. The hat itself is of silky brown beaver with a brown velvet crown, and the edge of the brim bound with the same velvet. There was, no other trimming save the white tulle veil which came quite over the face and was crosses]

VEIL ARRANGEMENT. at the back of the hat, the ends being brought round either side and tied in a bow a little to one side of the chin. The hair was dressed low, and the whole toilette, which was of em- broidered tuesore silk, was completed by this manner of adjusting the veil. The effect was very distinguished. Large picture hats are likely to be worn throughout all the winter, and are mostly of the Gainsborough style, in loiroir satin or drawn silk in either black or delicate shades, such as pearl grey or biscuit. They are in all cases trimmed with large ostrich plumes of the same colour as the hat itself, with sometimes the addition of some fine lace as a eapaie-prigße behind. There are also some very pretty toques composed of autumn foliage iii sevet atratiged in some instances as a foundation of coeam cloth. Lace and guipure awe intermingled with sable tails and late flowers, such as chrysanthe- mums and pompom dahlias, which in all their rich yet delicate (*lours harmonize with any sort of hat and gown. THE EXAMINER FRI 1) A Y OCTOBER. 3 1902

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The King will entertain the German Emperor, and the Xing of Portugal, this autumn, but the visits of these two Sovereigns will be strictly private, and will be sub- sequent to the royal progress through London on October 25th. On that occasion, the King and Queen will break the journey between North and South London at the Guildha 11, where their Majesties will take lunch. Successive Sovereigns have been entertained at the Guildhall from the time when Henry V and Queen Katherine dined there on their return from France,when the host of their Majesties was the famous Sir Richard Whittington, and we may be sure that the entertain- ment provided by the city Corporation will be worthy of the forthcoming royal visit. On the following day. Sunday, there will be the great thanksgiving service at St Paul's Cat hedral, so tbat Londoners will have no reason to complain of any lack of pomp and pageantry this year. Gordon Castle, where the Duke of Richmond has been entertaining the Prince of Wales, is one of the finest mansions in the North of Scotland. It was a daughter of the famous Duchess who is reported to have kissed the Gordon Highlanders into the King's service, who married a Duke of Richmond, and gave the ball which history records as having taken place on the eve of Waterloo. The estates thus passed to the Duke of Rich- mond, and the Dukedom of Gordon became extinct. Twenty-five years ago Lord Reacessfield was induced to revive it. in the Duke's favour, though the Marquis of fluntly also claimed the Gordon title, The present Duke of Richmond, who is in his eighty-fifth year, and served as aide-de-camp to the great Duke of Wellington, is thus the only subject of the King who is four times a Duke, his grace possessing the , strawberry leaves of Richmond in Englann, Lennox in Scotland, Gordon in the lJnited Kingdom, and d'Aubigny in France. The appearance and disappearance of a latter-day Messiah, has recently attracted a good deal more inter- est in London than the first air-ship flight across the Metropolis. There have been, at different times, scores and hundreds of self-proclaimed Messiahs and there are always many people ready to believe in such imposters. but it is steepes that so little interest should have been taken in the serial journey across London. It was very different when Lunardi made his first balloon ascent in London in 1784. Such was the excitement, that George 111 adjourned a Cabinet Council, and the people closed their shops in order to rush out and see the monstrous apparition in tbe sir. Lunardi became the popular hero of the day,and many articles were named after him by tradespeeple anxious to attract popular notice. Lady Arabella Romilly, wbo has been writing on the subject in a weekly journal, believes that a woman can find no happier time than when playing with her chil- dren in the nursery. She may shine, and dazzle in Society, she may attain a world-wide reputation as an adthoress, or on the stage, or in the concert room, but none of these ambitions, or their fullest realisations, says Lady Romilly, will give her the same peace and pleesere as she should find in her own children. Nor do they, but very of ten the fashionable woman ignores this old and simple tench, and regards children as vexatious hindrances to her own pleasures and conquests. As a rule there is much more affection and devotion between parent and child amongst the middle and working classes, than is the case in fashionable society. With those in the former condition the child is under the constant care of its parents, whilst among the "upper ten thousand " the upbringing of the child is largely entrusted to hirelings. The dressmakers of Paris, New York, Vienna, and London, each claim to be the rulers in the art of dress, and that they dominate the world of fashion. The fact is that the four capitals share this claim between them. There are, in London, firms established in Paris, and Parisian firms established in London, so that the fashionable woman gains nothing by going to Paris, to buy her gowns. She cannot do nearly so well for her- self as can the buyer of the large London establiehmen who knows her business and could offer her precisely Us same selection of toilettes in London at a much lowe tgure than she would herself pay in Paris. It is prob ably true that Paris and Vienna are pre-eminent in originating distinctive fashion, but Continental styles are often suitable to the Continent only, and both in this country, and in America, fashionable women have long ceased to he governed entirely by them. Blade toilettes showing touches of emerald green are being much worn. Care, however, should be exercised by those following this mode, as not every complexion can stand the proximity of such a 'bade. Black pro- mises to be almost a livery this coming winter, an excel- lent move on the part of Dame Fashion for women who , have comparatively little to spend upon dress. A trans- parent,cr semi-transparent black gowa worn over white or very pale shades, the trimmings in all cases matching the slips—has an entirely new effect on each occasion, and in this way, numerous ehanges can be*rung,at really little expense.ln these days of excellent patternsot is an easy matter to run up one's own slips, and from this stage the timid worker is usually soon brave enough to advance, and take her gowns in band. . . . .

The cache-peign, on many of the latest bats, almost 1 borders on the ludicrous, reaching as it does, well below the waist.. This is usually of black Chantilly lace, a fall of the same well covering the brim all round. The mode is being taken up with much fervour by women of the working classes, and is therefore not likely to be long considered by smart milliners. A novelty in hat pica is the fruit variety. A tiny pear, with one or two leaver a grape, an orange, and various other fruits in enamel, are taking the place of the jewel, either real or imitation, which for some time past has Leen the favourite form of finishing cif the necessary pin. The jewellers have not yet succeeded in starting any particularly new, and distinctive craze, such as the lor g chain dangling with charms, now of departed memory, but, there is a revival of the- heavy bracelet set with jewels or miniatures. It is also worn with filagree balls attached with chains' the balls being receptacles for the I favourite perfume of the wearer. Scent charms are also I made in the form of gold and enamel flowers, and another revival is the velvet bracelet clasped with jewels, and also the velvet throat band sparkling with gems. Little has been heard of ping-pong during what was meant to be the summer, but any hope that the winrer wculd find it dead and gone, does not seem likely to be realised. If one may judge by the many applications that are being made for permission to play ping-pong on licensed premises, it will be as popular as ever, when the dark days are upon us, and it has not only invaded restaurantP and coffee rooms, but even Atlantic liners I now have their ping-pong tables. It has also quite I eliminated the name of attic or morning room in house ' advertisements, since these announcements now usually refer to "two three, or four (es the ease may be) re- ception rooms," and a" ping-pong room." THE HOUSE ON BE HTLLI

BY G. B. BURGIN, (Author of "A Quaker Girl," &c.)

PART in

Isabel flew up to her sepulchral apartment, and almost danced into her riding habit. "Decidedly,' she thought, "I am increasing my knowledge oi life to a marvellous extent. People don't go riding about in Mayfair with handsome young men with- out a chaperon. Handsome young men don't save people's lives in that unceremonious way every day. And over all there hangs a shadow and a fear, &c. I don't know what the mystery is—it may not amount to much; but there decidedly is something. I seem to have lived years since yesterday. And everything is happening in a matter-of-fact sort of way which sets all my ideas of probabilities at defiance. It is only probabilities which are improbable. Is that a paradox? I don't know."

Suddenly a shadow swept over her features. She raised her arm, whip in hand. "I wonder," she said to the glass, "what would happen if a woman horsewhipped a man. People would say it was unfeminine. And yet I think I should like to horsewhip that recreant lover of mine—the man who made love to me because he thought I had money, and then quarrelled with me when he found I was penniless. It wouldn't have any effect on him. He would take it calmly, as a kind of return which he had deserved for his conduct. It isn't his desertion which stings me. It is that I should have loved—loved—actually loved a wretch like that! What must men think of women when we stoop to them in such a way? Really, it seems to me that when a girl meets a man with the exterior of an Apollo she doesn't even trouble to look inside to see if he is anything but a magnificent animal. When I think that I actually allowed that man to kiss me I could whip myself!" She drew on her gloves, and turned to leave the room. "Perhaps a struggle with the chestnut will restore my self-respeet. I don't, however, antici- pate any trouble. If a man can't ride her, it is probably because he hasn't light hands. I suppose we shall have a groom. We should hardly ride without a groom—even in Derbyshire." When she came down she found Geoffrey Hearne waiting to put her on the chestnut. That forward and fiery animal was dancing round and doing her best to knock the groom's hat off with her fore feet. The appearance of a habit acted like magic on her impetuous nerves. She came down on the path and pawed impatiently at the gravel. A touch, a swing to the saddle, and Isabel was mounted, the beautiful chestnut forgetting all pranks in her delight at the light, firm hand which held her. Then they reached the lodge-gate, turned to the left over the barren moor, and met the rush of the exultant breeze with that keen expectant shiver of delight which never fails a good horse and horse- woman, who feel that they will soon be on satis- factory terms with one another. Geoffrey Hearne came pounding stolidly along on his useful cob. Isabel had forgotten his existence. When she pulled in the chestnut—her cheeks flushed, eyes glowing, a wandering love-lock kissing her pretty neck—he was a hundred yards distant. Isabel sat there patiently awaiting his arrival, and patting the chestnut. " Ah," she said, presently, with a long drawn breath of delight, "my beauty, my beauty, you have taken the sting and the pain out of my heart! Henceforth I will live—live—live."

" If you do that again," said Geoffrey Hearne, who had caught the last word, "there is every probability you will break your neck ; " but he smiled admiringly as he said it. A beautiful woman on a beautiful mare! A long stretch of moorland, intersected by broad avenues of turf. A blue sky, in whicfi the little, floating foam.flecked.cloudlets followed and fastened on to one another. All doubtless very prosaic and commonplace. A man, coming in the opposite direction did not appear to think so. "How well that girl sits her horse," he said, wonderingly. "No sack of potatoes business about that. I seem to know her, too. Wonder who she is? I think I'd rather like to be that other fellow. They are coming this way." He lit a cigarette, and awaited their arrival. Suddenly, it fell from his lips. "By all that's improbable 1" he gasped; "Isabel Archer i" and, putting spurs to his horse, he galloped off in the opposite direction. Isabel put her hand to her head as if she had been shot. Then she rode on, strangely silent and reserved.

"What was it?" asked Geoffrey, noticing her silence. "Any one you know?" "A ghost from the outer world," she answered. "Let us ride on."

But the charm had vanished for the day. Even the chestnut's springy motion could not restore it. "I wonder if he feared me," thought Isabel, as they turned homewards. "I never thought him a coward before. Exit, Cohn Deep, once and for all." In the course of a few days Ishbel Archer ceased to feel anything embarrassing in her position. Geoffrey Hearne was the most masculine tame cat she had ever seen about a house. His duties appeared to be almost nominal. They consisted of daily rides and brief interviews with tenants, who began by petitioning for the erection of a new barn, and ended in great content with a gate or a pig- sty. At first they would insist on having the barn, but Geoffrey Hearne's firmness ultimately pre. willed. To use the words of a well•known Trans. atlantic poet :

===== 0002601_19021003_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
"Tie 's-as a toast detPrmined This quiet Mr. Brown."

Isabel found herself regarding Geoffrey Hearne with increased respect as time flowed on. Her own duties were very few. She arranged the flowers every day, transmitted Miss Summerby's orders to the servants, and took charge of the tea-urn. She was even becoming accustomed to her own room. Its darkness harmonised with her somewhat gloomy feelings. When she sat there in the even- ing, trying to discover what the Fates had in store for her, the darkness of her surroundings made an appropriate background. She told herself that it was absurd to imagine life could have nothing further for her because of one person's defection. Should it ever be in her power she would punish this false lover, not to avenge her own wrong, but for his crime against true love. The man had singled her out as the object of his attentions— followed her about for the whole season—made love to her in a thousand insidious ways—filled her whole life. For what? Only to leave it. And then, the paltriness of his motive. He found she had no money, and so abruptly forsook her. Angels might probably forgive so base an offence. It was rather too much to expect a mere human being to do so. No punishment could be too great for such vile treachery. She was alone one afternoon, about a month after her arrival at the House on the Hill. Geoffrey Hearne had driven oft with Miss Summerby to interview a refractory tenant. Geoffrey Hearne did not want Miss Summerby to go. The excite- ment of stimulating', the recalcitrant tenant's memory would give her a headache. But no; Miss Summerby protested that she didn't mind a head- ache, and so they had departed, the amorous Kinipple gazing mournfully at Mrs. Pilbeam as she helped her mistress into the landau. "It's no use to make one's self too cheap to-impudent creatures like men," Mrs Pilbeam had confidentially declared to Isabel as she turned-to the hall door. "If you do, it's a failure; they tramples on you—specially a man like Kinipple, wlio's got to be kept under; not but what he's a taking way with him When he likes. Let him die 16r me a little longer' and, perhaps, I may listen to him. You take my advice, Miss. Men are very well before marriage, but unless you drive 'em with a tight rein they are not nearly so meek afterwards." - -

Isabel sat down in a cosy corner of the terrace beneath the spreading shade of a fziendly acacia. She had a writing-case on her knee, and was think- ing over a letter to Mrs. Archer. At the end of the long gravel walk the neighbouring wood was gay with fox-gloves wildly dancing in the breeze. An occasional rabbit perkily scuttled across the path into the undergrowth beyond, or sat up mid-way and peered round in timid bewilderment. A portly thrush tugged at a reluctant worm, and dragged it forth to the light of day with a business-like air of a city man swallowing an oyster. There was a dreary quietude about the place. No human voice broke the stillness. Stay I Wasn't that the sound of horse's hoofs in the distance? Who could it be? Miss Summerby never saw anyone. No one would surely remember Isabel's existence, and come all the way from London to enquire how she was get- ting on in her new abode. She shut up her writing. case, and rose expectantly. "A, geatleman to see you, Miss," said one of the

housemaids, coming round the corner, and handing Isabel a card on a silver salver. Isabel took the card with feminine curiosity. "Captain Colin Deep," she said. "Did he ask for me?"

"Yes, Miss," said the girl. "First he asked for Miss Summerby, and I said she was out. Then he asked for you, and I said you were in. He's in the big drawing-room. Will you see him Miss?" "Yes," said Isabel, methodically gathering her belongings together; "I will see him."

She stood still for a moment in order to collect her thoughts. This accounted for the hurried dis- appearance of the mysterious horseman on the day of her first ride with Geoffrey Hearne. There had been many rides since then. She wondered if they were the cause of Captain Deep's presence at the House on the Hill that particular after- noon. _ . _

Captain Deep, a tall, fair man, with singularly handsome blue eyes, rose to meet her as she entered the room. There were distinct traces of crows' feet at the corners of his almost feminine optics. He did not appear to be more than thirty, and was exceedingly neat and well groomed. A long, flowing, fair moustache hid his mouth. For once in hi s life he seemed ill at ease. His manner had none of the characteristic sang froid of military men. He simply looked rather foolish, and very much ashamed.

Isabel coldly returned the bow. "Captain Deep,- I think?" she said, affecting to study the card in her hand. "I am afraid Miss Surnmerby is out. The maid said that you had asked for me." _

This was what is vulgarly known as a " facer " for the gallant Captain. There was a reason only known to himself and one other for his presence there to-day. He counted upon a scene— reproaches, tears, forgiveness, a blotting out of the past. Bygones had indeed been blotted out with a vengeance when this tall young woman, whose beautiful eyes had once looked into his with shy delight, could calmly consult the pasteboard in her hand as if doubtful of his identity. He began to doubt it himself. After the way, he had behaved, perhaps she was fearful of surrendering too readily. He knew that she had once loved him. She was not the first woman who had done so; his "fatal gift of beauty," as ,Toole says, had wrought much havoc in many feminine hearts. Still, he would be magnanimoqs, and heal the wound he had inflicted. He could afford to marry now, without relinquishing any of his own selfish pleasures. He stood hesitating for a moment, and then resumed his chair. What a lovely girl she was! Those dark shadows under the eyes were doubtless his work! They must be painted out. "Yes," he said, momentarily accepting the situa- tion, "lam a new neighbour of Miss Summerby's —have just come into some property about here— and reversed the usual order of things by calling on her first. She couldn't very well call on a bachelor."

"It was very good of you," said Isabel's cold clear tones, with just the faintest inflection of sarcasm in them; "but I am afraid Miss Sum- merby would scarcely appreciate such a departure from the conventionalties. She goes nowhere." "Indeed," said Captain Deep. I 4,lnderstood Miss Summerby was a comparatively New-comer. Hence my—my concession to sentiment. I am sorry to have missed her ; " but he did not go. To Isabers great annoyance the maid appeared with afternoon tea. She could not avoid asking Captain Deep to have some. He evidently did not intend to go without an explanation. She did not want an explanation; the man was hateful to her —she only wished to punish him. Possibly, Miss Summerby and Geoffrey Hearne would soon return, and wonder at his presence there. "Will you have some tea?" said Isabel, coldly. The maid handed it to him, and retired. Isabel drank hers with ladylike enjoyment..

What farces our -lives sometimes are—or trap- dies 1 Here were two people whosfew months ago were on the mostintimate terms now affecting to meet as strangers. One had behaved badly to the other. He was conscious of an avenging Nemesis, but did not know what shape his punishment would take. Somehow, he had realised that he was not going to escape scot-free. And the other? Oh, there was nothing about the other to call for comment. She was simply a very handsome girl, sitting backin a pretty lounge-chair, and drinking her tea with the air of one who was rather bored and too well-bred to let it appear. , Perhaps that was part of the punishment. After a few minutes, Captain Deep got up to depart. It was evident that he did not want to go. Isabel also rose. "May I hope to have the pleasure of seeing Miss Suinmerby on some other occasion ? " Captain Deep. Isabel's eyelids were slightly raised. "Surely, Captain Deep, it is usual to await a return visit, even in the country ?" "Oh, this will not count," said the Captain fatuously. " This was to you." " Indeed 1 You do me too much honour." "Yes, to you," said the Captain, eagerly. "Isabel, if you could only know how bitterly [have regretted my folly I" Isabel again glanced at the card on the table, as if to assure herself that she was not mistaken. "Really, Captain Deep, I cannot imagine why you presume to address me in such a way." "it is presumption, of course. I knew you would think it was. I can't explain it very well."_ _

"There is not the slightest necessity for any explanation whatever. I once had the somewhat doubtful pleasure of knowing—or fancying that I knew—someone who bore your name. Then I ceased to know that particular individual. He disappeared. His friends said he must marry money; he was reported to have proposed to Miss Jute, of Blackburn; and there was an end of him."

" I beg your pardon," said the Captain. " Per- haps I can supplement the story a little." "I am afraid, Captain. Deep, that I am not sufficiently anxious to hear about a matter of so little moment."

"You have no right," said the Captain, "to be unjust—to condemn any one unheard. What I have done has been bad and base enough. Now I have suddenly come into money, I hasten to make you the only paltry reparation in my power. Will you be my wife?" Isabel heard him without a thrill of emotion. A few months ago her heart would have throbbed with a wild delight. Now it felt like stone within her. "It may seem to you that I have no extenuating circumstances to plead," said the Captain. "Well, perhaps I haven't, but what little there are, for heaven's sake hear them." She stood cold, calm, and proud, and motioned to him to speak. "My allowance was barely enough for me to live. My regimental expenses were heavy. Horses and debts were swamping me. I met you. Report had it that you were rich. I loved you for yourself."

She gave a gesture of incredulity. " Ah, yes, you may disbelieve me; but it is so. Then matters came to a crisis. On the very verge of proposing to you, I learned that you had noth- ing, although your mother had plenty of money. To have asked you to marry me would have been the grossest selfishness. The rope was already round my neck, Miss Jute was my one chance. But I couldn't go straight from you to Miss Jute. I went away without a word to you—l dared not see you. My creditors gave me two months more. I went away to stay with some people. Miss .Jute was in the same house. People told you that I was paying my addresses to her; it was not true. I never said a word to her which might nothave been shouted out from the house-tops. I was nerving myself to do it when the telegram came which set me free. Norton Lees is my own ; I have two thousand a year to keep it up with—two thousand absolutely. There is enough ready money to pay off my debts. In a word, I am free ! Free of everything except dishonour. That clings to me ; it is a gangrene which eats into my flesh. I saw you the other day and dared not face you; but I have had to 4:1.3 it."

He paused, breathless. If he thought his passionate speech had moved Isabel, he was mistaken. _ . .

" Ail-that you say only proves more convincingly than ever that you never truly loved inc. If you had loved me, you would have known that I would have followed you to the world's end. When a girt lovea—(she caught her breath for a moment aE if in pain)—when she loves she does not question the why or wherefore. She does not ask for money, position, servants, fine houses. Ah, no! It is sc very different. Poverty, hunger, misery—all arE as naught. Life is one glad sweet dream of delight, for all the world's thorns become roseleaves at hex touch. Her soul is satisfied with the sweetnesE and majesty and holiness of love. Gl,r watches over her; the wni ve r se is a garden ;i4. one littlE smile, or word, or touch of love her guerdon for all the heat and burden of the day. Ah, no! You derade it, rower it into the—dust, ma Ye a mock of it, cast it off, and then would fain whistle it back to your call as a plaything for your idle hours. Your shame is your punishment. Some day, perhaps, it may be your redemption. Go II never wish to touch your hand or hear your voice

again." " But— "

"Good-day !" She rang the bell. "Order Captain Deep's horse, Mary." And the Captain rode mutely away.

"I hear you have had a visitor," said Geoffrey Hearne, presently. "He hasn't taken his tea." "Perhaps it was too Litter," she sea , She raised the cup in her hand, as if to throw it into the fireplace, but he took it from her with almost boyish reverence. "Wormwood would be swett from your hands," he said, and drained it to the dregs.

_ After the cup episode, a new life began ' for Isabel. Her self-respect returned. Here was another-man who evidently admired her, although he did not say so. Still, he knew that she had no money, and the knowledge did not appear to affect him in any way. It was evident that he also had no money. But had not the other man made that a ground for his desertion? There was something about Geoffrey Hearne which showed that he was fully alive to, the value of money, but that he did not make it his god to the exclusion of everything else. He must be regardless of money, or he would never seriously contemplate bringing out emenda- tions of Shakespear&44 But the emendations of Shakespeare had lately been neglected. Geoffrey Hearne had come to the conclusion that there were too many emendations already. He had recently paid a visit to the London Institute atoFinsbury Circus. There he had discovered a huge edition of his favourite poet, each volume of which was about two feet high, and broad in proportion. On every page of this edition he 'found about six lines of text, and that the rest consisted of emendations and notes 1 It seemed to him rather hard on Shakespeare that the poor poet should be as full of alterations as a last year's bonnet made over, and so, somehow, the great work was pitched on one side with an irresolution altogether foreign to Geoffrey Hearne's customary tenacity of purpose: Isabel had altogether forgotten about the mystery which she imagined to be contained in the House on the Hill. It ceased to occupy her, until one night she was awakened by a ray of moonlight dancing through the blind. It fell full OA her face, and dazzled her. She could not sleep. Then a mouse began to gnaw at the wainscot. The stable clock struck three. Should she get up and pull down the slats of the venetian blind properly, so as to exclude the light, or should she endeavour to go to sleep again? For some time she could not decide; in fact, she was not wholly awake, but in that half-aompolent state which gradually resolves itself into slumber if nothing happens to still further rouse the dormant faculties. But as she lay there every little noise conspired to disturb .The boards seemed to creak. The funereal canopy overhead actually shook once or twice as if moved by invisible hands. Well known objects in the room assumed fantastic shapes, and Made mocking motions to her in the changing moon- light. Her cloak, thrown carelessly on a distant chair, seemed to have some one in it. It almost moved. At times, Isabel fancied that it sat up, aslif very much at:home there. She tried counting a hundred, but broke off at thirty-five, she was too wide awake. Suddenly, she bethought herself that in a moment of youthful torture she had been compelled to commit to memory some fifty lines of "The Excursion." If they did not send her to sleep nothing else would. She tried, but could only remember ten lines, and the effort of trying to recall the remainder prevented their having • soothing effect. ARE YOU LIKE A STOPPED CLOCK?

A Stopped clock is "run down." Are you ? Summer's beat and close trying days, tell on the business man,and still more on the woman in the home. Loss of appetite, loss of energy, a feeling of constant tiredness, frequent headaches, and inability to go your old pace—these are the signs of being run down. The cure, if you wish to apply it without wasting time, and running up a heavy doctor's bill, is Chas. Forde's Bile Beans. This is no mere theory. Miss M C Porter, of Little Hale, (Lincs.), says, " I was quite run down, and became subject to frequent attacks of neuralgia. My nerves were shattered my strength had gone, indigestion bad crept on me and acute pain after even a light meal, and now and again a bad bilious bout made me quite • done up.' lltly doctor did his best, but somehow be could not get me well again. Then I took Bile Beans and to my delight they gradually restored me to perfect health." Any ' chemist will supply them at one and three half-pence or two and nine per box. Don't take any inbidltute.

It appPared that she went home on Tuesday night in a state of drunkenness, and her husband refused to admit her to his bedrcom. LORD FRANCIS Bore AND MISS MAT YOHE.—AmonZ the decrees Mei made absolute by Mr Justine Jeff in the Vacation Court, on Wednesday, was that in the case of Hope v Hope and Strosig. The petitioner was Lord Henry Francis Hope Pelham Clinton Hope, brother of the Duke of Newcastle, and the respondent was the well-known actress, Miss May Yale. The parties were married iu November, 1894, and travelled together for some time in America and elsewhere. Afterwards the respondent left her husband and joined the co-respond- ent, Colonel Strong, formerly of the New York Militia. Proceedings for divorce were commenced by Lord Francis Hope, and a decree nisi was pronounced by Sir Francis Jenne on March 21st this year.

DRIVING WITHOUT ON REINS AT WARRINGT.

A Carter Censured.

At an inquest held in the Coroner's Court,Warrineten, on Monday evening Mr Brighouse severely censured a carter in the employ of the Whitecrose Company, for not having reins attacked to a horse which be was driving. The inquiry was relative to the death of Richard Worrell, 74 years of age, a wire worker's labourer, employed by the Whitacre:se Company, Ltd., who was knocked down by a horse and furry on the 15th of August, in Milner-street. Margaret Wortall, daughter of the deceased, said that at half-past eight on the moraine, of the Friday in ques- tion, she heard that her father had been run over in Mileter-itreet. She went there and saw him removed to the Infirmary. He died there on September 26th. Ellap had visited him several times, but be had not referred to the accident. Her father was very deaf. Nurse Garnett stated that the deceased was admitted to the Infirmary at about nine o'clock on the Friday morning, suffering from a compound fracture of the left arm. Be died at eight o'clock on Friday, September 26th. John Blease, the driver of the tarry, said he was nro- I ceeding up Milner-street on the merning of August 15th. He was driving towards Froghall-lane with a horse and harry. A young man named John Stockton was walkieg lalongside the vehicle with his hand on the abaft. Wit- leess was in Milner-street, but had no reins to the horse. The works " buzzer" sounded, and the horse was frightened. It went forward and knocked the deceased down. Witness was sitting on the !wry and had gone about ten yards along the street. He was going from the galvanising shed to the stable. The Coroner: If you had no reins, you had nu control overithe horse. They talk of danger with meter cars and cyclists, but there is more danger with a horse and vehicle on the road without reins' What have you to say for riding without reins?

Witness : I am very sorry. The Coroner : I should think you were. The acci— dent would not have happened if you had had reins. It is perfectly ridiculous. You know this is as near man- slaughter as possible. Many a jury would send you for trial for manslaughter. You cannot go wandering about the highway with a horse and lurry without reins. It is monstrous. Witness !stated that when he was working with the horse and lurry about the works he did not use reins. If he went into the town with the horse he always used reins. The Coroner: But you were going along a street where the people have a right to pass. If you were only in the works you ought to have reins. Joan Stockton said he was leaving the works at 8.30, and the" buzzer" was making a noi<e. The horse and lurry were on the left hand side of the road, and the de- ceased was walking in the centre and was going in the same direction. The horse got frightened and it jumped up, and the shafts of the furry struck the deceased. He I was knocked down. and the wheels passed over his arms. Witness ran at the horse and shouted.

By the Coroner : The driver Blease was sitting on the shafts, and witness was walking along talking to him. The Coroner, addressing the jury, said the facts were not disputed. This man was driving along a street 1 where the public had a right to pass and repass. He I did not care whether it was one or five yards long. There the driver was, sitting on the shafts, and driving without reins. The law said that persons using the 1 highway should exercise every care. Tee law put an obligation upon the driver, otherwise something woe bound to happen. They must exercise sere with skill and judgment. If the man Blease had done so, then it was a pure accident ;if not, then he was negligent. If so, to what degree ? He did not say it was to such a degree as to warrant him being sent for trial, but if the jury thought he had been negligent and deserved cen- sure, then they must censure him. After consideration, the foreman said the jury thought the death was accidental, but that the driver should have been at his horse's head instead of sitting on the shafts' considering he had no reins to drive with. The Coroner, in censuring the driver,said he was sur- prised the firm was not'represented. The driver ought to have bad reins. The authorities were very particular about fining motor car drivers who could bring their Inanimate steeds to a scandstill almost itnmediately,and they were also very fond of fining cyclists who could also control their machines, but here was a case of a horse and lurry being driven in the public street without rains. He (the driver) had better tell his firm they must have reins to their horses or they would be getting into trouble. An old man had lost his life in this case and it was important that deaf people should be pro- tected as well as other people. The driver then left the box.

THE EVENING CONTINUATION SCHOOL.—This school re-opened for the winter session on Monday evening in the National School. A large number of pupils presented themselves for admission, over 40 being enrolled which is consilered very satisfactory for the first evening. An attractive list of subjects has been arranged, and it is hoped to add a sick nursing class. During the evening Ithe Vicar presented the bronz3 medal awarded to Miss Annie Mather, for her success in an essay competition promoted by the Canadian Government the subject being "Canada." The class is in charge of Mr and Mrs Blackwell, while for sir; subjects Mr H Woodier,. of Warrington. has been engaged. CYCLING CLUE.—LADIES' 1/AV.—Absut 40 members and lady Mends took part in the club's annual "ladies day" run on Saturday, this being the last official tun of the season. The day was everything that could be desired and the destination was Mr Wright's farm at High Legh. A 'bus was chartered to convey the ladies and those gentlemen who were unable to cycle,and High Legh was reached about four o'clock after s delightful drive. An excellent tea was served by Mrs Wright, after which a criaket match was played—ladies V. gentlemen. Dark- ness set in and the match was declared a draw. At 7 30 the return journey was commencetand after a splendid run via Appleton Thorn ,headquarters were reached about 15. Various songs were given by the members, and completed a very enjoyable outing.

WARRINGTON T. RUNCORN-A DRAW.

The keen rivalry between Warrington and Ruacorn drew a crowd of about 6.000 tis the Warrington enclosure on Satur- day. From the borne side Ir herwood was an absentee, his knee net being sound enough after the match the previous Saturday, while the suspended Bdmunds was replaced by Duckworth, Runcorn wore at f nil strength and brought a large followleg of spectators. The day was b.autifully fine, and the conditions generally favoured a fast game. &r B Ashton of Werneth was the referee, and the teams were as follaws

Warringtoa—Kaight, back ; Fish, Hockenhall, Dickenson, and Harris, three-quarter backs ; Duckworth and Burgess, half-backs : Roberts, Boardman, W Swift, F Swift, Taylor Edmondson, Morrison, and Forster, forwards. _

- _ Runcorn—Houghton, back; Flannagau, Wa-der, Butter- worth, and Jones, threequarter backs ; Richardson and Jolley, halfbacks ; Walker. Farmer, Lightfoot, Woods, Reece, Moulton, Higgins, and Williams, forwards. Runcorn lost the toss, and Farmer kicked if. Duckworth replyin g. was charged down, but Forster stepped in and pre- vented the Linnets from making any further headway. The resulting scrimmage sent the ball to Jolley, but the latter was too closely watched to get away. Some exciting forward rushes followed. Runcorn's fine punting kept Warrington on the defensive, Knight being rather unsteady during the first tea minutes. Burgess passed to Duckworth, and bet. Fish. but the latter, after a phicky attempt to get away, was floored by Butter- worth. This was really the first attempt at passing since the game began, but another immediately followed, and had not Butterworth intercepted a pass intended for Fish, the last named might have modes good attempt for the goal line. The next minute Warder received the ball,and getting splendidly through the opponents, he passed to Butterworth, and the latter bluffed the Warrington backs splendidly. He punted the ball, and followed up, Knight making hisl mark close to the line. Again the ball was worked to the opponents' end of the field, and Burgess get clearly away, but fell down just at the moment when a pass to Harris might have resulted in a score. A fine dribble by Burgess followed, whits!' Walker stopped by falling on the ball. The game, which had been very fast lup to now, slowed down considerably. For some time little lof interest occurred. Hockenhall at length got the ball, and 11 got through a host of opponents. He passed to Harris, but the latter was promptly tackled. Butterworth looked dan- gerous when he intercepted a pass, but Dickinson brought him down. and Walker at length got the ball at top speed. and hal almost a walk in. but to his disgust he found that he was offside. From a round of passing, however, Jones got over, but was called back, owing to the pass being for- ward. Runcorn had the hardest of husk the next minute, for Riehardson kicked across to Flaenagan. and Harris, who marked him, fell down. Flaanagan had not a man to stop him, but fortunately for Warrington, just before he reached the ball it rolled in touch, a foot frets the corner flag Half- time arrived just afterwards with no score. F Swift restarted, and the first scrimmage was formed on the Runcorn half of the Said. Seine exciting play took place and Knight by following up well all but scored. A free kick by Houghton put Runcorn en the offensive, but again Morrison. Edmondson, and Swift dribbled well to centre. Warrington were now playing with great dash and Fish was all but in. The wing man was very closely shadowed, and Jones was penalised for getting offside to do it. Fish just failed at the shot. Still Houma were hard pressed. and Harris and Foster rushed the ball over the line. Foster seemed to have touched the ball behind the post, but the referee refused to allow the try. Harris distinguished him- self again by a splendid bit of individual play, for getting away with the ball he dodged Ulnae opponents in succession and kicked to Houghton, who managed to save at the Run- corn quarter flag. Neither side had up to now made too much use of touch, and though the game was not very scientific, it was fairly interesting A fine round of passing by the Warrington backs gave the ball to Harris, who beat both Warder and Flannagan, but he could not get past the redoubtable Honghton. A free kick by Fish sent the ball over the line, just under the posts, and Jolley was compelled to touch down before he could clear. The drop out did not give mush relief to Runcorn, and the game was in their half, Runcorn were having by far the worst of it, the Warrington forwards improving as time went on. Burgess and Fish got in some telling runs, and Houghton failing to gather the ball properly from a big kick,fell in touch. From the ensuing scrimmage, Runcorn were penal- ised, and Fish again just failed at a clearly easy place. A number of long kicks enabled Runcorn for the first time for twenty-five minutes to get a footing in the Warrington half, and Jolly tried to drop a goal, but without avail, and the next minute as the result of a dashing run by Fish,Warring- ton were again pressing. The pressure was maintained, and at:the elose Runaorn only just escaped disaster by Houghton finely tackling Harris close to the Runcorn line, Final.—Warrington nil, Runcorn nil.

HER FAVOURED HAUNT. Lost Lady Found by a Newspaper.

The disappearance of a young lady who had long been a familiar figure in Portsmouth led to fears for her safety, and some interest was awakened locally when the Ports- mouth Times announced that she had been discovered safe and sound. Now married happily to a naval carpenter at the dockyard, Mrs Hayles readily accounted for her absence from familiar haunts. She had been ill, but thanks to Dr Willtams'pink pills for pale people health and strength have returned to her. When sixteen years old," she said, " I was so delicate and anee nic that none of my relatives believed I was long for thls world. I went to several doctors, and was also an out-patient at the Royal Portsmouth Hospital, but nothing seemed to do me any good. My head was always aching ; I

,e whiter al u I thinner every day, and could eat nothing, ho wever e aipting. I became weaker and weaker, until I could not move about, and it was thought I would not recover. "My cousin bought me a box of Dr Williams' pink pills, and persuaded me to take then,. I had given up all hope from medicine, but, to my surprise, after a few doses of Dr. Williams' pink pills, I found myself feeling much better. My appetite improved, and my energy came back, S 3 that after the fist box I seemed a different woman. Before taking the pills I had no appetite, and was so despondent that I used to sit in the churchyard, and wonder how son I eh mild be laid in my grave I " Now,' she added smilingly, "my appetite is wonderfully good, and I can eat almost anything. I have gained considerably in flesh and colour, and those who know me are surprised at the change in me." Inereased and purified blood-supply explains this and many similarlcures. Anmmia, is often the forerunner of consumption,is at the bottom of many womanly diseases, backache, shortness of breath, hysteria, and the like, and Dr. Williams' pink pills are noted fcr their cures in Such cases. The pills are only genuine in the registered wrapped, with Dr. Williams' name. Tney are for men, women, and children.

Mn. ilayles' case (her address is 27, Grafton-street, Landport (Portsmouth), is instructive and typical.

THE EDUCATION BILL.

Free church Council's Appeal to the

Town Council.

At a well-attended meeting of the Executive Com- mittee of the Warrington and District Free Church Council, held in Bold-street Chapel vestry, and over which Mr C E Parker presided, the following resolution, proposed by Mr E ENGLAND, and seconded by Rev J F MATTIIEWg, was unanimously passed :—" That this meeting of the Executive Committee of the Warrington Free Church Council appeals to the Warrington Town Council to declare ita determination not to administer the proposed education law (should it become law) un- less an appeal has been first made to the country ; and this meeting trusts that every member of the Corpora- tion who sympathizes with the principle of public con- trol in the administration of national funds will support this proposal." It was decided to send circulars to the„ candidates at the forthcoming municipal eleatione as to their attitude towards the Bill, and another public meeting to protest against the measure is being arranged.

WARRINGTOI

University Extension -Zectures. Arrangements have been made with the Oxford University Extension delegacy for a Course of six lectures to be delivered in the Co-operative Hall, Cairo-street, on the " Prose Writers of the lath Century." The lecturer will be Mr J G Powys,M.A., (Corpus Christi College, Cambridge), and the first lecture will be delivered on Thursday evening next. The subject will be " Lamb." The lectures will be continued every alternate Thursday evening, when the writers under discussion will be " Hazlett and DeQuincey," " Lawlor," "Carlyle," " Macauley,"and ".Raskin." Tickets for the course (including copy of syllabus) are one shilling. Special arrangements have been made for lending the works of the writers under discussion to holders of lecture- tickets.

Seed news continues to arrive daily from Denmark, where Queen Alexandra is enjoying a well-earned and well-needed holiday after a most trying time, which, despite her perennial beauty and brightness of disposition, cannot but have told upon her physically. First, there was the royal entertaining and the Courts prior to the Coronation, then the King's illness, and, finally, the postponed Coronation: and at the end of this protracted and trying season her Majesty was not in the best of health. Every autumn, almost without exception, since the daughter of the Sea-Kings first made her home in England, now nearly forty years ago, it has been her habit to visit her parents, King Christian and his late Consort, who has been BO d4ply mourned since her death four years ago this week: and no more united family exists than the royal house of Denmark, which, though spread all over Europe by marriage, always manages to reassemble at Copenhagen towards the end of each summer. During such times the royal household for the most part live an unostentatious family life amongst themselves without any state ceremonials, beyond such festivities as a gala lunch to celebrate Prince Christian's birthday, which took place last week: and in no royal circle is greater simplicity observed. Queen Alexandra, for instance, goes practically unattended by any suite, except for Miss Knollys and an equerry. Her stay is now drawing to a close: and she will be back in England in a few days to take part in the royal drive through town. His Majesty is due at Sandringham next Saturday, where the week after he will be visited by his great friend, the King of Portugal. Their Royal Highnesses the Prince and Princess of Wales have, also, left Scotland after their first visit to their new home at Abergeldie for York Cottage, aniihis week spent a few days in town at York House, making arrangements for their impending move into Marlborough House. They will, however, make York Cottage their headquarters for the next three months, as the accouchment of the Princess of Wales is expected next month: and, except for the State visit to London on the 25th and 26th inst., the King and Queen will spend most of the autumn and early winter at the favourite Norfolk home.

This week Shipka Pass, which five and twenty- years ago was the scene of the fiercest battles between the Turks and the Russians, has been the centre of festivities: and in memory of the fallen and the great Russian victory a Greek church has been opened. The Grand Duke Nicholas of Russia and Prince Ferdinand of Bulgaria were amongst the notable gathering who met at the wild Pass to celebrate the victory and to open the church, which was designed by Professor Vosnesenski, who has introduced the double Russian cross prominently into the minarets. Shipka is a pass in the Balkans. on the side nearest to Roumelia, about fifty miles north-east of Philipopolis, and eighty-seven miles south-west of Rustelauk on the

THIS WEEK.

Danube. The Russians, in 1877, having been driven from Bournelia, retreated to Shipka Pass, where, under Generals Gourko and Radetzky, they withstood the desperate onslaughts of the Turks under Suleyman Pasha, the chief fighting taking place between August 20th and 27th and Septem- ber 9th to 17th. After fifty thousand men had been sacrificed General Todleben was successful in enclosing the Turkish army at Plevna, and on December 10th they surrendered unconditionally. By the Berlin Treaty of the following year Bulgaria was constituted autonomous. Hence the present rejoicings and the great memorial church in the wilds of the Balkans. The manifesto of the Boer Generals and their appeal to the civilised world has called forth a storm of criticism on every side: and even by their best friends it can hardly be considered a wise or tactful move on their part. It was obvious from the account of their interview with Mr. Chamberlain officially published that they came to England expecting enormous con- cessions beyond the terms they had agreed to at Vereeniging : and doubtless they were considerably chagrined by the attitude of the British Govern- ment on the subject. It is to be hoped, however,

THE SIGNATURES OF THE THREE BOER GENERALS

TO THEIR NOW FAMOUS MANIFESTO.

that the feeling aroused on both sides will soon subside and the incident be forgotten, amongst many other " unfortunate " ones, in the best in- terests of South Africa.'' General Botha seems to be the most moderate and sensible of all the Boers in his ideas; awl he may be trusted to do his best to make matters go more smoothly upon his return to England with his two colleagues, which is ex- pected to take place about the time of the opening of Parliament. In view of any lectures the Boer Generals propose to give and any subscriptions they hope for towards their funds, their manifesto was not the wisest move: and the least said, the soonest mended. One generous American, Mr. Henry Phipps, last week presented General Botha with £20,000 for the immediate needs of the Boer widows and orphans, coupling with his gift one or two simple conditions to insure its only being used in a way Great Britain would approve sf and one which could not be considered un- friendly to us : and in accepting it and the condi- tions General Botha again made use of the frankest expressions of loyalty.

tieneral sorrow will be expressed at the death of Mr. John Latey, who passed away at the end of last week after a long and painful illness at his residence, South Villas, Camden Square. He sus within a month of his sixtieth year, t.aving been born in London on October 30th 1842, a few months after his father had launched the "Illustrated London News." He was educated with a view to a journalistic career, which he commenced with the first issue of the "Penny Illus- trated Paper" at the age of nine- teen. Since then he has con- tinued his con- nection with this popular little journal without a break, keeping it always in the front rank of popularity.

MR. JOHN LATEY, EDITOR OF THE " SKETCH " AND THE "PENNY ILLUSTRATED

PAPER."

I n connection with with it he founded the "P.LP. Showman," and did Parlia- mentary sketches for the Illustrated London News," as The Silent Member." Ile also acted as assistant editor of the older journal for some time: but the principal post of his life was the editorship of the "Sketch," whicn he took over in 1899, and conducted with great vigour and spirit. In addition to his journalistic work, he wrote a number of novels; novelettes, comediettas and other minor works. He was one of the founders of the London Press Club and a fellow of the Institute of Journalists. He will be greatly missed by his many friends, as he was regarded with the warmest feelings of liking and affection on account of his general kindliness of demeanour. The funeral took place this week at Highgate Cemetery.

Recently there have been several interesting additions to the Royal Zoological Society's collec- tion in Regent's Park. Amongst these is a hybrid zebra, presented by the King, who 'has always evinced the greatest personal interest in the Zoo, and has frequently made presentations to the Society. Another valuable presentation comes from Colonel Mahon, D. 5.0., the reliever of Male- king. Who is now Governor of Kordofan in the Soudan. From his new post he has sent three giraffes, two cheetahs, and two secretary birds, which were brought home by Mr. Thompson, the superintendent of the Zoo. All thesl3 have gone to the Regent's Park collection, except one giraffe, which went to Dublin, in Colonel Mahon's native land: while another similar one was pre- sented to the Khedive en route. Of the two at the London Zoo the larger is a female about twenty months old, and the smaller a male, just over a year old. They were led down to the coast this summer and temporarily placed in the Zoo at Gizeh. They were shipped by the Momba.v.ea. and had a very poor passage: but sinoe reaching England they have picked up considerably. Another new arrival is a purple-faced monkey, who has just been added to the inner room of the monkey house, amongst the rarer specimens of his chatter-

THE LATEST ADDITIONS TO THE ZOO.

ing tribe. It comes from Ceylon and is technically

termed the " semnopitbecus cepbalopterus." It is.' a small monkey of dusky hue, and its face would at first sight be called black by the majority of observers : but in -certain lights there is a purple bloom upon its almost hairless countenance. It has a few stray white hairs in its bristly moustache, but is otherwise unmarked with wnite. Its fellow in captivity is a Campbell's monkey (Cereopithecus campbelli) from Africa. These form three very interesting additions to the fine collection at the Zoo, and will tempt many lovers of natural history to ...make a journey to Regent'e Park.

Whether one sympathises with Mr. Kensit'a attitude on religious questions or not, everyoner cannot help being horrified at the murderous assault to which he was subjected at Liverpool towards the &lose of last week. He had been addressing a mass meeting of Protestants in connection with his son's imprisoment at the Claughton Itinsic Hall, Birkenhead, and left escorted by a stong band of police or Woodside Ferry, by which be intended to cross the Mersey to Liverpool. The trowd was not as hostile as had been anti- cipated and com- paratively fe w people had gathered at the ferry. Mr. Kensit duly alighted from the electric car to walk to the ferry gates when suddenly he fell with a cry: and it was found that he had been wounded over the left eye, a fright- ful cut having been inflicted by a sharp chisel about a foot long an d weighing about two pounds This had been VRO WAS MURIiEROL':•LY AS. hurled at him IIAULTED AT LIVERPOOL LAST from same little WEEK. 4i,tance and had inflicted a terrible wound to the left eye. At first it was feared that it might prove fatal : but he has been progressing favourably; at the Northern Hospital, to which he was conveyed ij,n an ambulance. It' is now hoped that not only will he recover, but also that the sight of the eye may be saved. In connection with the affair the police have arrested a young man, named Makeever, who has been charged with feloniously wounding Mr. Kensit. Naturally in these enlightened and civilised times such a deed has created the greatest sensation throughout England, especially as M. Kensit has of late years taken such a prominent stand in religions matters..

AFFECTION

CURED

Dear Sir—Some two years ago I met with an acci- dent to my back, injuring my kidneys and affecting my liver, the result of this being that my health was completely shattered, and although I was under medical treatment for some time I despaired of ever enjoying good health again; in fact I became very despondent and even prayed for death. My weak- ness was so great that it unfitted me for work, but I heard of Webber's wonderful medicine " VITADATIO." and have taken six bottles, and it is with real pleasure that I inform you that my health is completely restored, and I am much stronger in body and able to do my work with pleasure, and enjoy life, which was Impossible before. "All thanks to Webber's VITADATIO." May many poor sufferers test and prove its value as a health restorer as,l have done. Qratefully yours, SILAS PERRY. 66, Lord-street Stockport, England, Jan. 17th 1900.

MANCHESTER.

Corresponienee Invited, Write for Teetimoniale.

The London "Daily The Mines and the War, obeying the establishe, principle of sensational halfpenny journalism—that the public likes to be told as a fact what it desires to believe—has been circulating some very comforting rumours aboat the cost of the war. At one stroke of the pen,we are told, Mr CHAMBERLAIN is about to relieve the country of half the burden of the late war. No less than one hundred millions are to be inscribed in the national ledger as a book debt against the two new Colonies, while a loan of fifty millions 14 to be raised at once on the security of the mines - This really sounds most alluring. There is, to be sure, no reason why it should be false. The mine are quite capable of bearing even so large a charge as this. Mr HAYS HAMMOND told US in 1899 that as a result of the war they hoped to gain millions per annum in increased dividends. Net e penny of that unearned profit rightfully belongs to the financiers. It has been earned by the British people. Now this sum alone would suffice to pay the interest ou a loan of very neatly 100 I millions—if there were no ether new charges for the Transvaal to bear. But unfortunately there will be our vast garrison to pay for, including an expensive constabulary paid at the rate of 53. e day. Then salaries have to be found for all the young men from Oxford whom Lord MILNER has imported to rule the,country by theory and inspir- ation. IL fact, we are spending more under every head than the Boers did, except upon education. All this has to be paid for before a war debt can be thought of. But there is a yet more delicate question to be faced. Are we prepared to carry our Imperialism so far as to impose a tribute on a distaxt colony? Above all, are we ready to revive the situation which created the American War by imposing taxation without representation? Jo- hannesb-trg will not submit quietly to be bled iLk our interests, at the mere dictation of Downing street and Lord MILNER. it is easy to retort that it is only the mines and their cosmopolitan masters that will have to pay the tax. But it so happens that the people of South Africa also have their eyes on these mines. They want to use their superfluous; wealth for local objects—to build rail- ways, to irrigste the land, to improve educations and to finance the various schemes that will be necessary in order to repair the devastations and losses of the war. The moment we attempt te enforce taxation we shall unite South African opinion against ourselves, and revive the cry "Africa for the A.frikanders," which was so often on Mr. RHODES'S lips. Until he saw that he must call in the Empire to help him to crush the Boers, after his own Raid had failed, he was bent on the "elimination of the Imperial factor." Unless wo are very cautious we shall find that the war, instead of strengthening our position in that part of tho world, has made it quite impossible. Lord MILNO. will not succeed where GEORGE 111. Failed.

was, is visibly weaken- ing. It never excited enthusiasm save its a small and extreme clerical party. Popular support had none—no one, for example, has ever attempted to organise such a meeting to approve as the West Riding of Yorkshire arranged to attack it. But at length the revolt of the Tory party has begun in earnest. Now it is a prominent Liberal- Unio that, like MT. PITT LEWIS, who attacks it ; now a Conservative like Mr. SUTTON, the leader of

he party in Reading; and at length even Bir- mingham has risen in the persons of some of the foremost of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S own personal ad- herents. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN himself, who seemed ready some months ago to defend the Bill in a half-hearted way, has written to a correspondent to explain that it is "not in his department.' When that astute electioneer abandons his friends the end has already begun. It is the history of 1885 which repeats itself. Most significant of all, however, is a speech by Sir W. WALROND, in which that ex-Whip turned Cabinet Minister assured his constituents he would faith- fully represent their views to Mr. BALFOUR, and then went on to suggest a compromise. Some sort of compromise is bound to come, but it will not be on Sir W. WALROND'S lines. He thinks that if Nonconformist pastors were allowed to give religious instruction to the children of Dissenters in Church schools, while Anglican clergymen were permitteA to invade the Board Schools, every difficulty would vanish. That proposal may be well meant, but it simply ignores the real objec- tion to the Bill. It would still leave a sectarian majority with the absolute control of the only school that exists in upwards of 8,000 parishes. It would in no way meet the real Liberal objection, that the Bill provides for the expenditure of public money with th 3 merest shadow of public control, and without the direct representation of the ratepayers. So long as this sectarian majority appoints the teachers and fixes every detail in the daily life of the school the atmosphere will be clerical. On the headmaster, as Sir JOHN GORST admits in an article in the "Nineteenth Century," not religious teaching only but the whole spirit and nature of a school depend." Whatever compromise may in the long run be accepted, the committee which appoints that headmaster must include at least a two-thirds majority of public representatives. Most of these may be Churchmen; the headmaster may always be a Churchman. There need, how- ever, be no fear on that score. There is no feud between Dissenters and Churchmen as such. What is intolerable is that a non-elected body which generally represents not the Church, but a narrow minority in the Church, should wield the power to perpetuate and disseminate its own bigotry and prejudice.

rA new Viceroy has entered Dublin in state, welcomed as an official by officials, but ignored by the municipality, which used to send its mayor to , meet his predecessors. Lord Roberts met with a ' kindlier reception when he entered Pretoria ; but then if Pretoria was under martial law, war was really in progress. Dublin, on the other hand, is subjected to coercion at a moment when her , streets are unusually crimeless. It is difficult to see anywhere a ray of hope. The idea of com- promise in the land war has failed, for the great landlords have one and all refused to have any- thing to do with the suggested conferences. And I now one cannot open a newspaper without reading of the arrest of some Nationalist Member of Par- liament. Conceive for a moment what the feeling in England or Scotland would be if in distriet after district the chosen representative of the people were to be arrested simply for uttering sentiments with which all of his constituents agree, and then tried without a jury by a removable magi- strate. The thing is inconceivable, but if it really occurred there would be a revolution. In Ireland it is an incident of the daily life of the people. It is interesting,in view of the fact that the Liberal League is for ever urging us to wipe the Irish Question off our slate, to find that a typical Con- servative life Mr Sidney Low, the editor of the k"Standard," has now begun to preach Home Rule L•V... in the pages of the" Nineteenth Century." One (,- could wish that Lord Rosebery were as good a 1 ? , Liberal as this Tory editor, who writes :—" It is ‘it impossible to contend that our present relations with the vast majority of the Irish population are ~ satigactory. We cannot go on for ever in the squalid quarrel, which threatens the Imperial P.' Parliament with a recurrent loss of dignity and the saaifice of such efficiency as it possesses • nor can we consider the material condition of Ireland, and the painful figures revealed in the last census • returns,as anything but a melancholy reflection on our capacity for statesmanship."

There can be no sort of doubt that the auttimn campaign on which Lord Rosebery and his friends are entering will be a purely sectional adventure. Lord Rosebery cannot complain that the party as a whole has turned a cold shoulder to him,or failed to give him opportunities for co-oporation. He was invited by the National Scottish Liberal Organization to address a great meeting in Edin- burgh, and refused. Again he was invited by a joint committee of the Liberal organizations of the four Edinburgh constituencies, and again he refused. And now it is announced that he will, after all, speak in Edinburgh, but under the auspices of the "Liberal League." Apparently Lord Rosebery prefers to be the undisputed chief of a schism rather than take an honoured place among the leaders of a united army of progress. But it is important that Liberals should realize how completely he has chosen to isolate himself, and how absolutely this deplorable result is of his own seeking.

"The custom of public-house treating is now justly held to be one of the chief causes of intem- perance in Ireland," says the rule card of St Patrick's Anti-Treating League, and the remark applies with equal truth and force to this country also. We have no such league here, but if there is a necessity for such an organisation in the " disthressful country" there is an equal, if not greater, need here. Treating is equally prevalent on the commercial as on the social side of life, and

in both cases it of ten does incalculable harm.

Many a young man has ruined a promising com- mercial career through falling a victim to the seductive good-fellowship which he imagines is in- here nt in treating. Lord Roberts himself showed L 1 s keen appreciation of the dangers of this harm- by habit our Soldiers commenced manifesto he issued at the time ceoseadrenced to return from the war. He was goodwill of the y earnest in his request that the public towards the " Tommies " should not be shcfvfuri had the treating, and we hope his warning the. effect of deterring some, at least, of those Should dduals cursed with this false kindness. We " welcome a branch England, for it is of the Anti-Treating Leagueueinan our firm belief that more done for the furthering of temperance in this direction than in would any other. The number of total abstainers ou increase rapidly once treating came into disfavour, and there can be no question that the anti-tr eat- lug movEment, new though it is, is phenomenally bright with possibilities for temperance. ---0.--. 4 TABLE & HANGING- LAMPS.—J. Diem was a sitting of the Court both morning and evening. The business was purely formal. GOOD TRAIPLARY AT RUNCORN.

The Runcorn Forward Lodge 2444 held its weekly Session in the Ashridge-street Mission on Monday evening last, being opened bv the CT at eight. The programme for the evening was in charge of the Widnes Consolation Lodge. Songs, duetts and re- citals were rendered by Sisters Smith, Ashton, Prescott and Bros Smith and Jones. At Runcorn Police Court on Friday, before Mr F J Norman (in the chair) and Mr J Ockleston, Margaret Derbyshire, of Runcorn, was brought up on a warrant for disobedience of a summons charg- ing her with having assisted in the management of a disorderly house at 20, Regent-street, from April to September. Detective-Sergeant Gregory who apprehended the prisoner at Salford, and PC Bratt proved the case, and the women was Cam- mitted to gaol for two months. THE B. MI. C.
