RUNCORN WATRRMAN'S -SAD DEATR.

Mr R Dobson, district coroner, conducted an in- quiry at the Runcorn Police Station on Friday after- noon into the circumstances attending the death of Henry Owen, a Waterman, who lived with his stepmother at 93, Surrey-street, Runcorn.—Ann Owen, widow, 93, Surrey-street, said the deceased was her stepson and lived with her. He was 34 years of age. About 14 years ago he received an injury to his foot and had always complained of it. He complained very much last week. There was a big white blister on the foot and she treated it with poultices. He said the foot gave him a great deal of pain and he complained terribly of his head. He left home on Monday morning,but only worked for an hour. It took him an hour to walk from No Man's Land to his home. On Thursday night he rambled in his talk, speaking mostly about his work. His breathing was very hard. He got up at six o'clock on Friday morning and shortly after- wards went back to bed. She took him a cup of tea and he spilled half of it. She was downstairs when she heard him tumble on a chair, which he broke. She ran to him and helped him up. He staggered out of the room, and, slipping from her grasp, fell downstairs. He lay there face down- wards and beat his head on the sofa and table legs. He snored and foamed at the mouth. She tried to hold him, but could not. His 'sister went for a doctor, but he died a few minutes later. That was at twenty mitaLtes to seven. Detective-Sergeant Gregory said he had known deceased for about 10 years. He was a very steady decent man. Witness saw the body soon aft€r death. Over the left eye there was a large cut. The face was very much swollen underneath the right eye. The nose was bruised and black. There was a quantity of blood on the table leg. He had seen Dr Robinson, who said that death was caused by a fit due no doubt to the injury to the foot. The jury returned a verdict of "Death from natural causes"—The Coroner said that Sergeant Gregory bad asked him to say that there vvas no handrail on the stairs. A juror: There are a great many houses in Runcorn in waich the stairs are not provided with a hand- rail.—The Coroner: And elsewhere too.—Sergeant Gregory : These stairs are very awkward and steep.

RUNCORN NOTES. [BY' A CORRESPONDENT]

As will be seen from our advertisement columns, the annual distribution of prizes will take place on Monday at the Victoria-road Board School. It is to be hoped that Runcornians will note the fact and that there will be a good attendance. The Evening Continuation School, under the excellent manage- ment of Mr J F Moulton, are really doing a first class work in the town. They make a youth who has left the day school to keep in touch with educational matters at a critical period of his life and just when there is an exceedingly strong temptation,to let elementary learning drift into disuse and obscurity. Last sessiOn there were several middle aged men, whose learning had been neglected in early youth, who managed to secure prizes. This in itself is sufficient to demonstrate the value of the schools. Every educationalist must wish them a most successful session.

The Free Church Council, mainly owing to the energetic action of the Secretary pro tern Mr Walter Millington, is about to organize a series of meet- ings to condem the present Education Bill. I understand, further, that the electorate who will be supplied with printed circulars, are to be requested to write to Sir J T Brunner, the member of the division, urging upon him the necessity of voting against the third reading of the Bill. Now the Free Churcernen of Runcorn if they honestly desire to scotch this iniquitous measure must close up their knees. Let the weak-kneed ones return to their homes in dishonour or let them stiffen themselves back and act a part that will absolve them, even though the battle be lost, from playing the traitor to their children. If the timid ones are fearful let I them, for their own credits sake,keep their fears to themselves. It cannot be gain said that the time for compromise is passed. The boats have been buried and there cannot be any turning back. The unconstitutional attempt to trample on the liberties of the people, to institute a corrupt reigarhy composed of the priest and the aristocrat is in itself sufficient justification to resist to the bitter end even by unconstitutional means the pay- ment of the ' church rate." If the end be imprison- ment, or forfeiture of material goods it is only an incident similar to many in the long and stern his- tory of "Independency." If our principles, when put to the test are not worth suffering for,then we have no part or parcel in all those glorious reforms that have made England the England of to-day If Runcorn—a town which proudly beasts of its Nonconformity and of its Liberalism be now false

to her traditions then the town cannot be reckoned I RUNCORN TECHNICAL INSTITUTE. to be any longer in the van of those who have pioneered the cause of liberty and reform.

Some years ago at an immense cost the Urban ounctl obtained an order sanctioning the provision f electric light power in the town. The Council ave not, however, yet moved in the matter and be order is on the point of expiration. It would be very interesting to know why the Council applied for the order, and have since refused though it cost a considerable sum to obtain permis sion, to lay aown an electric plant. Was it because the Council were afraid that some electric company might step in and intro- duce electricity, and that the gas works— & concern in which some of our leading Ituncornians are interested—would suffer, or was it because they leaped before they looked, and atter waras found, when considerable expense had been entailed, that the scheme was impracticable. I think more than one ratepayer would like to have these pertinent queries answered. Perhaps some gentlemen will be kind enough to lighten our darkness on this subject.

PROPOSED COUNTY SANATORIA.

I The Health Committee reported that the Cheshire County Council having furnished copies of confer- ence of local authorities with reference to the estab I lishment of Consumptive Sanatoria desire to be I informed whether the following resolution set forth at the end of the said report meets with the ap- proval of the Runcorn Council : Resolved—'• That in the opinion of this conference, but without in any way committing the several authorities repro- sented,it is desirable that a sanatorium or sanatoria I should be established in the county for the treat- ment of consumption and that the County Council should take the necessary steps under the Isolation Hospitals Acts, with a view to an order being made including all the sanitary districts in the county a hospital district, for the establishment of such sanatorium or sanatoria and at the same time obtain the consent of the Local Government Board to include " phthisis " as an infectious disease within the meaning of the Infectious Diseases Notification Act."

The committee recommended that the County Council be informed that this Council approves of the establishment of sanatoria for the treatment of consumption, but that details would have to be supplied before it could commit itself to any scheme.

Confirmed on the motion of Messrs Jones and Walton.

AN ANNOYANCE TO PROPERTY OWNERS.

Mr Imison said he wished to draw attention to a practice which was becoming very prevalent in Runcorn. He referred to the chalking of public places in the town by mischievous boys. It was a practice which put Property owners to considerable expense. He asked that the Council send some communication" to the Superintendent of Police asking him to instruct his officers to keep their eyes open and to arrest anybody who might be found acting in such a manner.

ELLESMERE STREET MISSION WARRINGTON.

Sale of Work.

With the object of raising ;' venm sufficient to clear off the existing debt on this mission for ground rent, and if possible, forming the nucleus of a fund for the building of a new chapel, a sale of work promoted by the " Charles Garrett" Temper- ance Union was opened in the mission on Thursday last week. The stalls had been very tastefully arranged and were laden with a variety of wares, the following being the stall-holders Temperance Union stall (ladies), Mrs Whitmor, and others; Temperance Union stall (gentlemen) Mr John Ellison and others ; flower stall, Mrs Fred Cox and others ; refreshment stall (platform), Mra Pendlebury, Mrs Sherburn, Mrs Wood,Miss Barlow, and Mrs Gregory ; children's stall, Nirs Williamson and children ; fancy stall, Mrs J W Moss. There was also a fish pond in charge of Misses Minnie Whitmore and Patty Taylor and an exhibition of curios under the direction of Mr Horton and Miss titregson.Mr R Wood gave character delineations and other attractions included gramaphone selections, instrumental and vocal music, and a galvanic battery. Mr Llewellyn had charge of the cloakroom. The sale was opened on Thursday by Mr J B Isaac, of Frodsham, Wr J Dodds presiding. After devotional exercises,

The Cnerneres, stated the objects of the sale, and Said he thought they were also met to promote a cause which woule commend itself to the warmest sympathy and most cordial support of all wile loved their fellow- men. The work of the temperance reformer could only be commended from whatever side they viewed it. To seek to prevent and to stem the tide of intemperance,to rid their country of its pernicious drinking customs, to I lift their poor fallen brothers and sisters out- of the power and tyranny of the drink demon and to place them upon the platform of redeemed manhood was surely a work worthy of their earnest and enthusiastic support. He hoped the ff ert which would be launched that day by his friend Mr Isaac and continued for the remainder of the week would be abundantly successful and that the result would encourage the members of the Charles Garrett Linton Co prosecute their endeavours with unfaltering will sod undiminished faith. In ark- ine Mr Isaac to open the sale the Chairman said he believed that gentleman was the frunder of the mission —(Applause)-30 successfully arried on there. He was also a gentleman who, for about a quarter of a century to his (Mr Dodds') knowledge had maintained the most honourable traditions of a Christian man of business and of worthy citizenship. He (the chairman) was glad to be asscciated with him and trusted thet he would long be spared to Warrington to help on every good work and to show what good men were. (Applause.) Mr J B ISAAC, who was warmly received, said he knew the Chairman had a warm heart for every good cause, and was willing to help those who helped them- selves,. That was what the people of Ellesmere-etreet Mission were doing. He need not say that it was a very great joy for him to come and meet his old friends and fellow-workers in that great and good cause. He felt touched to see the stability and firmness with which some of the young people had stuck to the work of the mission. Some had been engaged in it since its forma- tion ten or twelve years ago. Mr Isaac went on to allude to the commencement of the old Sunday school in Buttermarket-strret, which was the forerunner of the missfen,and to the outdoor services which'were held in th ' neighbourhood. At that time he did not think he should ever witness such a scene in the course of a few years as that which was presented that day. On looking round he saw the result, not of gifts of monied men, but the result of earnest, devoted, loving sacrifice on the part of those who had not a great deal of money, but had will- ing hearts. He need scarcely say that '.that was as ' glorious a work as it was possible to or gage in. He wished people would think about it seriously. They talked a great deal about patriotism and imperialism, but he said that the man or woman who helped to purify the neighbourhood in which he or she resided, and to raise the country in purity and righteousness were the best patriots that the nation could possess. Continuing, Mr Isaac said he thought the owners of cottage property n that neighbourhood ought to give a shilling each towards that sale of work. Owing to the work of that mission the landlords bad obtained better tenants, the rent was more secure, and the value of the property was therefore increased. Mr Isaac concluded by wishing the sale complete success. On the motion of Mr J BARLOW, seconded by Mr JAMES LINGAED, and supported by the Rey G El CAEN- SON (the new superintendent), the Chairman and Mr Isaac were zordially thanked for their services. In responding the Crreraineer expressed the hope that Mr ;Damson's ministry would be successful. The business of the sale was then proceeded with. The proceeds for the day amounted to £22 is U. FRIDAY'S PROCEEDINGS.

On Friday afternoon the sale was opened ty Mr C E Parker. President of the Free Church Council, Councillor James Evans presiding. The CHAIRMAN said he was hoping that a better state of things might soon be reached in connection with the Church of Christ. He referred to the great interest ' which people took in snort, and said he supposed it cost something like £30,000 per week to " run " football in this country. That would be a considerable item for the Church of Christ if it were turned into that channel. He contended that if people gave to the Church accord— ing to their means there would be no necessity for bazaars and sales of work.

In opening the sale Mr C E PARKER said that of all the a:encies that were calculated to raise man there was no power on earth equal to the power of Christianity or to the love of Christ. There were many agencies and good works, to which he would wish " God-speed" affecting the surroundings and the circumstances of men and women. It was a very sad thing that as be got to learn more of Warrington be found :how the surroundings of the men and women and little children were of such a debasing and lowering character that there seemed little chance of much elevation. But there was no doubt that the power of religion went deeper into the nature of men and women that any other elevating force. With regard to the temperance question, there was nothing ;that stood more in the way of the onward march of our Lord Christ than intemperance. There waa a time, even in his recollection, when there was a lack of that unity of purpose, of work, and of effort between temperance , work and Christian work and Christisas were wont to say that temperance workers were placing temperance in the place of the Gospel. Happily that time had gone and it was now recognised that if there was to be effective Christian work the way must be paved by temperance work and the obstacles in the way of the spreading of the Gospelmust be cleared away before the Gospel could march onward to its full and final triumph. Mr Parker in conclusion wished the sale every success. On the motion of Mr SHERRI:MN seconded by the Rev R WiLiosts REES. a vote of thanks was accorded to the Chairman and to Mr Parker for their services, and the , sale was resumed.

Saturday was the concluding day of the sale of work promoted by the " Charles Garrett ' Temperance Union in connection with the above mission. The opening ceremony was performed by Miss Bessie Dutton, of Moore, Mrs Carnson, wife of the newly-appointed Super- intendent of the circuit, presiding. Miss DUTTON, said her duty was a very short but pleasant one. Those associated with the bazaar bad had many hours of work and bad made great sacrifices, but 1 they were glad that the result so far had been good. Those present were working very hard for their beloved little church. It was only a small building,but just as the friends at Moore likrd their little chapel there, so they in Warrington loved theirs, and realized what was said in the Psalms "I had rather be a door keeper in the house of my God than to dwell in the tent, of wickedness." She hoped God would bless them more and more in their temperance work, as temperance was the best prepara- tion for the work of Godliness and bringing religion into the heart. She was glad to be present if only to wish them " Godspeed." It was with much pleasure she declared the sale of work open. (Applause.) Mr EDWARD HOUGHTON proposed a vote of thanks to Mrs Carps= and Miss Dutton for their services.

1 Mr CHARLES DRINKWATER seconded, and remarked that the friends in connection with the chapel were working people striving to work for God, and the present was a humble effort to get rid of the existing debt on the premises. It was very acceptable to receive the sympathy of those in a higher Btatiom in life, and it showed that the religion of Jesus Christ could make people humble. They hoped soon to have the present building re-placed by a substantial house of God. The motion was carried, and Mns CARNSON, in reply. said she was willing to do what she could for God's cause.

Miss DUTTON, in her reply, alluded to the everlastin Saturday afternoon football matches, which upset man things in these dais of traffic. She had that afternoo to travel in the guard's ran, for all the first crawl carriages were crowded, and there did not appear to be any chance of her reaching Warrington until the guard said she might travel in his van with two other people. The receipts on Saturday were £l9 153 Lid. which added to the first dav'e proceeds of £22 7d 4d, and second day's of £l7 17s 811. brought the grand total up to £6O 03 2d.

During the evening a message was spoken by Mr John Rama into the gramaphone to the Rev W Hollings- worth, who first proposed the sale of work, but who is now in the ministry at Garnish, Newfoundland, where the record will be forwarde d. The concert room was packed at every performance to hear this message re- produced and was the source of a large revenue.

Annual Report.

The following report upon the Runcorn Technical In- stitute for the year 1901-2, was submitted to the Urban District Council at the monthly meeting on Thursday, by the Technical Institution Committee. Day School.—The number •of pupils attending the school at the commencement of the school year on Sep- tember 16th, 1901, was 79. In the Secondary School there were eleven girls and forty-six boys ; total fifty- seven. In the Preparatory form there were thirteen girls and eleven boys ; total twenty-two. For the corres- ponding term of the previous year there were 91 students. Of the 79 students nine held connty scholarships, 15 held local scholarships, six held Brunner third year scholarships, and 49 were paying students. Two students were presented last December in the Cambridge Local Junior Examination. One passed in honours, clots 3, and one satisfied the ' examiners. During the year Runcorn was made a small local Centre by the Oxford Delegacy. Thirty-one candi- dates, all students in the school, were presented for examination. Fifteen passed, being 48 per cent. ; one senior third class honours, four junior, and ten pre- liminary. On the suggestion of the Inspector a playing field off Greenwas -road has been secured and a fives court in the playground of the school has been provided. A room in the Institute has been set apart for the use of girls during the dinner hour or after school. The students' fees amounted to £295 7s id, compared with £377 7s 63 for toe previous year. The Board of Education's grant amounted to £329 3s 23, as against £331 03 93. Of this grant, £259 3s 2d was for the elementary courses, representing £5 for each student, and £7O for the advanced courses, repre- senting £5 16s 83 for each student. The grant works out at £5 3s per student on the whole of the Secondary School. The Cheshire County Council contributed £l5O being an increase of about £3O on their previous grant. Evening Science Classes and Art Schosl.—Science classes: The following science subjects were taught:— Applied meshanics, building construction, theoretical and practical inorganic chemistry, practical plane and solid geometry, machine construction and drawing, magnetism and electricity, practical mathematics, physiography and steam. Practical mathematics was substituted for pure mathematics at the request of a number of the science students. There were ten classes In the elementary stages of science subjects and five in the advanced stages. In the case of geometry the ad- vancea and honours stages were taken concurrently with the elementary in respect of two students. The percentage of passes is 63 compared with 34 for last year. It is gratifying to report that a student passed in part I. of the honours stage of chemistry and that one passed first class in the advanced stage of practical chemistry, one passed first class in the ad- vanced stage of machine construction and two passed first class in the advanced stage of magnetism and electricity. Sixteen obtained second class passes in the advanced stages of the foregoing subjects. The number of students on the registers was 172 as compared with 151 last year. One hundred and twelve students were examined and 63 were successful. Technology, Sao : Classes were held in carpentry and joinery, cookery, dressmaking. French and shorthand. One hundred and sixty students were on the register as compared with 125. The number examined was 81 and of these 67 were successful, giving a percentage of 84. One student obtained a first class in carpentry and join- ery. School of Art: The number of individual students was 48, The number last year was 57. Forty were presented for examination. Nineteen passed, giving a I percentage of 47 as compared with 22. Most of the students took freehand drawing of ornament only there being few taking model drawing, ' drawing in light and shade and the more advanced sub- jects. The total number of individual students in the , Institute was 314, there being 192 males and 122 females. Last year the total was 308. A gas engine and dynamo, with switch board and electric wiring and lights have been provided at a cost of about £l7O, for demonstra- tions in electricity, machine construction, applied mechanics, • Sze. Towards the above sum Sir J T , Brunner con:ributed £5O, the Highfield Tanning Com- pany £5O, and the Runcorn Co-operative Society £3 3s, the balance being a charge on the funds of the Institute. An honours board has been placed in the principal corri- dor at the cost of Sir J T Brunner. The grant from the County Council in respect of the evening work was £3lB with a further grant of £29 Oa 103 for attendances of students taking the advanced stages. The grant last year was £393. %This large falling off is due to the fact that the grant is made on the basis of population and the census of 1901 WAR used. The grants of the Board of Education were £65 64 103 for science and £35 19a 53 for art (£7B 7s 83 and £5O 184 63 last year.) The fees of the students amounted to £63 134, an increase of £2 18s 63. The gentlemen mentioned in last year's report who promised to contribute to the local scholarship fend have paid in their donations. The debit balance is now £lll 4s 63. Annexed is the statement of' income and expenditure and tables showing results of examinations. —JOHN WILSON, chairman ; E MansuaLL, secretary. A table follows showing the subjects taught and the results of the examinations. The statement of accounts for the year ending March 31s.t gives the following items :—Ezpenditure : By balance owing to treasurer, 1901, £37 23 43 ; salaries of teachers and officers, £1,052 is 63 ; apparatus and books, £53 17s 83 ; prizes, £l3 133 11d, and other items making a total of £1,617 03:5d. Mr Wilson moved that the report be adopted. Si. JOHN'S CHITHCHMOMPLETION FUND.

A social evening and supper in aid of the shove fund, was held in the Mission Hall, Earlestown on Thursday evening. There were almost 250 persons present. Dancing commenced at seven o'clock, Messrs Woodcock and Brown providing the music. At intervals the Rev J C Matthews sang "The Skipper "and " The Soldiers of the King.' NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS,

Rugby Football.

[By Ovalist.] Runcorn retained their unbeaten certificate on Satur- day though they must be accounted lucky to withstand the continuous onslaught of the Warrington men in the second half. The "Linnets" were always tenacious, determined fighters,however, and in the past they have obtained all the possible points in many a game which they have had far the worst of on play. Such has been the case at Warrington, when by a sudden spurt and a smart drop kick the Runcormans have triumphed though apparently beaten. Nothing quite so galling happened at Wilderspool on Saturday, though I feared it when Jolley dropped for goal on the only occasion on which Runcorn reached the home quarter after half time. As a matter of fact there was no score, notwith- standing the great efforts put forward by both teams. It was a disappointment to the homesters for with the least bit of luck they would have won. It is not often for instance, that Fish misses two fairly easy goal-kicks when so much depends upon them, as he did on Satur- day. One little goal would have won the match, as anyone could see with the proverbial "half an eye."

Before the commencement the majority of the 6,000 people present would have laid slight odds on Runcorn seeing that they came with an unblemished certificate I and a full team, whereas Warrington's record was in- ferior, and the team was weakened by the absence of such men as Isherwood, Edmunds and Hallam The substitutes were all triers, however, and they acquitted themselves well. Isherwood's presence on the left would, I think, have made a big difference, for smart and plucky as Hockenhall is, he cannot play the centre game for Fish anything like so well as his skipper. Fish and the team are considerably reduced in effectiveness by the absence of Isherwood, who, it is fervently hoped, we shall soon have back again, notwithstanding pessimistic tarradiddles.

The game itself was fast and furious right through, as befitted a battle between keen rivals; and, moreover it was not a bad sample of Rugby football under the present touch-rule. There was a lot of touch-kicking and scrummaging, but the excitement attendant on all the operations prevented play becoming dull. In com- parison with the game as it might have been, however, there was much to be desired. But for Warringthn's determination to play the back game, and their persist- ence in running and passing,the match would soon have degenerated into a scramble, for the Runcornians were less inclined to open out. They showed their best form in the first quarter, when they quite over-powered War- rington with fine kicking and some dashing work both fore and aft. Once Knight, the home full back, had found his nerve, the team bucked-up in a fashion which kept Runcorn on the defence right to the end.

What bad luck the Runcornians had was in the early stages of the game when Jones got over the line after good passing—which was unfortunately forward—and Flanaghan lost a certain try through just failing to secure the ball before it rolled in touch at the corner. Harris was meanwhile full length on the grass as the result of a stumble so that the young "Linnet " would have had the easiest of tries had he been on the spot the hundredth part of a second sooner. From this point Runcorn had scarcely a look in.

Warrington's most surprising display was forward where the somewhat despised e ght showed what they really could do when nerved to the task. They got possession three times out of four and it was a distinct novelty for Runcorn to be so badly beaten in this department. Warrington certainly ought to have won considering the chances the front rankers provided, but , there was not quite the necessary smartness behind to overcome such a defence as Houghton, Jolley, Butter- worth and Co., set up. Burgess and Duckworth did not work well together—they never could—but each was good individually, the Flixton youth doing some exceedingly fine things. Dickenson was erratic when attacking, while Hockenhall and Fish made little pro- gress. Harris was by far the best of the home three- quarters, and against a leas stalwart tackler than Sam Houghton he must have scored two tries at least. His speed and dash are serving the team well, and he bids fair to have a most successful season, while Fish is handicapped sadly by the absence of his regular centre. Warrington's third line has by means got into its swing yet.

Hallam still holds aloof, and from what I can gather he is unreasonable. Moreover he is unwise, for Knight is coming on fast and in the second half of the game on Saturday he gave a really sound display which was not ' even inferior to that of Houghton, who seems almost as good as ever. Runcorn seem to me stronger in defence than attack,though Butterworth and Jolley still possess the knack of going for and getting near the line. Flannagan and Jones both did well, but they are hardly the equal of Fish and Harris. Walker was the best of a set of forwards who got badly left in scrum- mage work.

Brighouse Rgs Hunslet

The Yorks clubs have made rather a poor start in the Competition, only Halifax and Bradford being included in the first eight. Swinton's record is easily ahead of any other, and their performance at Broughton on Saturday stamps the Lions as a team considerably above the average. Though 'tis early yet to predict the winner of the League tourney, I fancy Swinton will be in the running right to the end, when tliey should finish on top. The Rangers are their most serious opponents in Lancashire, Salford and Oldham both being considerably below par.

tlT'Fhe South West Lancashire League up to date is as follows : Points

P. W. L. D. For ngst [Pte. Warrington Runcorn 2.. 1 . 0 . 1 ... 10 ... 0 ... 3 Wignn Leigh St. Helens 1 ... 0 1 ... 0 ... 5 ... 18 ... 0 Widnes

Warrington have another stiff task to-day when they visit their keen foes, the "gallant youths " of Batley. Last year each side won at home, the margin of points being in favour of Warrington, though they were badly beaten at Mount Pleasant. This time, they say they are going to win both the encounters with the champion cup-fighters. Much of to-day's confidence is due to the fact that Isherwood is captaining the side once more, his injured knee being reported well again. This is joyous news to all supporters of the club, for "Dan "is as capable as he is popular. I hope he comes out of the fray safe and sound, for with his regular assistance Warriugton are certain to make a creditable show this season. Duckworth has been dropped, and Hockenhall will partner Burgess behind the pack. These two work as well together as any pair of halves Warrington could select.

The team will be : Knight, full back ; Fish, Isher- wood. Dickenson and Harris, threequarters ; Burgess and Hockenhall, half backs; Boardman, Taylor, Morrison, Roberts, Edmondson, J Swift, W Swift, and Forster forwards.

To day's engagement is the first of three succes- sive ones away from home, Salford and Halifax being the other two, so that Warrington fairly have their work cut out for the month of October. Leigh at home is the final match of the month, and on this day it looks likely that Warrington will have their hest men repre- senting their county against Cheshire. Pessimists may say that Warrington will get no points this month, but I hope for something much better than that. The result of the game at Batley to day will be very significant as regards the possible fruits of the month's work,

One of these days we shall see the Warrington team in all the glory of new jerseys, for they have been on order some time and are expected "every minute." The new jersey is in the style I sugg,estsd a year or two back—i.e. the same colours with the bands running round the chest, instead of downward. Warrington Will, I am sure, look a vastly smarter lot when the new "togs" come.

The teams for the Lancashire County trial match have been picked, and no fewer than three Warrington men appear among the probables, viz., Dickenson and Fish, threequarters, and Boardman forward,while Harris is among the reserves. The intention is, apparently,to play club-mates together in the centre and wing posi- tions, which is, perhaps, the best way of securing effectiveness. .So far, however, Dickenson and Fish have had very little experience of playing together,and what little they have had has not been altogether a success. If they are to shine on behalf of the county they will require to play together a time or two for Warrington. As a rule though, Dickenson goes too much "on his own " to 'be the right man to accompany Fish,

Warrington Athletic Club.

The above club commenced the season on Saturcla7 last when eighteen members turned out for a run front headquarter. the Bridge Inn.) As it was the first run this season the trail was dispensed with,all the runners turning out in one pack, R Price acting as pace and Mather (captain) whipper in. The course taken was through Arply fields, Walto a and Moore, and then back by way of the Ship Canal to the Stag Bridge. The pack then made straight for home down the New Road and after a spirited run the first to arrive was H Hil/ followed by J Hall and R Price and J Phoenix fourth. The members of the above Club (amongst whom are the most noted athletes in the district) have had a most successful season on the path the number of prizes they have won being 43 of a an aggregate value of £lBs.They have every prospect of doing better this season as in addition to all the old members over twenty new men have joined the Club.

CONCERT IN PARR HALL.=

The second annual concert given by the War- rington Apollo Male-voice Choir in the Parr Hall on Monday evening attracted an audience which filled the building in every pert, the performance of an interesting programme under such circumstances, thus fitly opening the winter's musical:season. The concert was chiefly noteworthy as marking the progress made by the choir during the past twelve months. It was most refreshing to notice the great improvement which has been effected in this interval in the choirs quality and balance of the tone,the flexi- bility of the massed voices, and the degree of their responsiveness to the baton ; and the joint conduc- tors Messrs Harry Berrey and Tbos Brough are to be warmly: congratulated on the success of their efforts to achieve results so creditable to one and all—a success amply attested by the fact that the choir has won prize distinctions in several recent competitions. What is now to be guarded against is, rather a tendency to go to extremes in the observance of certain marks of expression which by being interpreted too literally defeat in a measure the purpose intended and affect the listener disnleasurably as harsh contrasts always .do. An instance of such inartistic exaggeration may be cited in the choir's rendering oF "Watch her, kindly stars " where a climacteric crescendo is abruptly followed by a aeries of pointed syllables sung pianissimo. But what was more than any- thing else conspicuous in the choir's singing is Lb e evidence it gives of a deeper and more intelligent insight into the pieces attempted. In the perform- ance of the part song already named as in "Annie Laurie,"" The Spartan Heroes" and "The Comrade's song of hope," there was much that was highly praiseworthy,the earnestness shown in these pieces demonstrating that the choir is working on right lines, and that to excel in style and method is the one thing for which all are striving. It is not sur- prising tnat the audience was frequently enthusi- astic,in expressing approval of these good things to the extent of occasionally demanding repeti- tions. Mr. Berrey's Operatic Company collec- tively distinguished itself in some familis yet interesting excerpts, but, individually as soloists, some of its members were less satisfactory. Mr Berrey himself evinced good musicianly qualities in his song" The Sailor's Grave," and in " Mary of Argyle" given in res- ponse to an encore, and Miss Annie Dennett like- wise proved herself a competent and tastetul vocalist in her rendering of a somewhat unworthy theme "The songs my mother sang." Nor would Miss Ethel Parr have been less successful in " Killarney" had she but opened her mouth wide enough, failure to do so seriously handi- capping vocal effort, otherwise promising if not entirely adequate. The male soloists Messrs Hareblett and Topping were chiefly disappointing in that the singing of each was tame to a degree. For a doleful rendering of "The Curfew" something might be said, though there seems nothing intrinsi cally gloomy in the sentiment of this good old song, but why so rousing a song as " The King's Champion" should be declaimed in so spiritless a fashion passes one's comprehension. Mrs Berrey bore the brunt of accompanying skilfully and with appropriate unobtrusiveness, ?being most ably assisted in this :arduous task by Mr Albert Trow. As the inevitable humourist at this class of entertainment Mr Wilson Black- ledge did the best possible in mightily pleasing the audience in a number of sketches, the wit and humour of which, if not very profound, was at least innocent and sometimes illumined by flashes of spontaneity.

PRESENTATION TO A 'FORMER BARLESTOWN CRICKETER.

News as to the doings of Mr F P Clare, who for so many years performed yeoman service for the Earlestown Cricket Club, is always welcomed by local enthusiasts of the summer pastime. Mr Clare left Earlestown a few years ago, and has since been playing with Nantwich C.C., who are members of the North Staffordshire League. During his first season with the Cheshire Club he was well placed on both the batting and bowling averages, and he has gone on steadily improving until last season he was one of the most prominent members of the eleven, in fact he came out top of the batting aver- ages. For that performance he was last week pre- sented with a bat given by Mr Travers Pickmere (of Nantwich), the prize having been offered at the commencement of the season. The presentation was made at a supper held at the Wilbraham's Nentwicb, about 50 sitting down to an excel- lent repast provided by the host.Mr G Harding occupied the chair, and presented the bat to Mr Clare. He said none could forget Mr Clare's remark- able efforts when such a sensational victory was gained over their keenest rivals, Crewe Alexandra. On that occasion, he obtained six wickets for eight runs, truly a splendid achievement. For that accomplishment Mr Clare received no prize but that incident would never be forgotten by those whe were followers of local cricket. He had great pleasure in asking Mr Clare, to accept the bat which he than handed to him. On the bat was the following inscription on a brass plate :—" Nant- wich Cricket Club. Presented by Travers Pickmere Esq., to T P Clare for the highest batting average 1902." He hoped to see Mr Clare often using it and making some good scores With it.—The rest of the evening was spent in the usual manner,songs being rendered by the musical members of the company As is well-known in Earlestown, that Mr Clare has anlexcellent tenor voice and daringithe;evening be sang" Mary of Argyle " and " l'll sing thee songs of Aralry," both songs being loudly applauded.

SHOCKING CASE OF OVER-DRIVING NEAR WARRINGTON.' At the Warrington County Petty Sessions, on Wednesday, Charles Reed, of Liverpool, was summoned for ill-treating a horse by over-driving it at Riaton on September 9th—P.C. Clarke stated that at 9.30 p.m. on the date in question be saw a brown pony attached to a cart standing in Manchester- road, Rixton. Defendant and two other men were asleep in the cart. Defendant said he could not get the old horse any further. It was •• done up." The animal, which was very old, was bleeding from the mouth, owing to the bit jagging, and was standing on three legs. It was sweating very much and seemed to be completely exhausted. Defendant said he had driven the horse too hard. Me was driving it from Liverpool to Manchester with a load of furniture. He added that the animal was thirty years of age, and it had not been in a stable since leaving Liverpool. It had been on the way nearly forty hours. Witness told the deiessdant to take the horse to a stable, but it was so weak it could not draw the vehicle into the yard, and had to be pushed in. Witness subsequently saw the owner of the horse, who said he did net want the defen- dant to take the horse, as it was not fit for the journey._ Inspector Print, R.S.P.C.A., said he examined the animal, which was very aged awl in moderate condition, on Septem- ber 10th. The collar was still wet with perspiration, though it was 24 hours alter the offence. When the horse was abnroduwghetakonuetssoef every reeledssttaebpleititwent. A HOME TREAI MENT FOR DEAFNESS.

Dr Ludwig Mond's Generosity.

I understand, says the London correspondent of the " Birmingham Daily Past," that Dr Ludwig Mond, P.R.S., of the firm of Messrs Brunner Mond, and Co, founder of the Davy-Faraday Laboratory here, has promised the Royal Society the handsome donation of £6,000 totvards the final expenses of completing the society's useful "Catalogue of 'Scientific Papers." This compilation, I may say, is the outcome of a movement dating back nearly 40 years, and which bad its inception at the Glasgow meeting of the British Association in 1835. The idea was to set on foot a compraheneive and authorita- tive catalogue of the writings of scientific men in Europe and America, beginning from the year IEOO. Originally, the work was printed and published at the public expense by the Stationery 0151 e, but this mode was changed a few years ago by the wish and under special arrangements with the Treasury, The Royal Society now intends to close the series on completing the indexing of scientific papers to the end of 1900, and Dr Mond' s gift is to be devoted to this particular purpose.

Ridgway. The last-named offioer might be called on 'of our veteran soldiers in South Africa because he bad served there go long and with such credit. The toast was cordially drunk. THE EXPICRIENCES OF THE THIRD CONTINGENT . A NOVEL MUSICAL INSTRUMENT.

In raeponding,Oaptain Feieceetiall said that on behalf of the third section he begged to thank the Mayor and townspeople for their kindness in entertaiaiag the volunteers that evening, and aloe for the kind way in which they had looked after the wants of a great many of the men whilst they had been in South Africe. He also thanked them for the insurance pelicies and field glaeses which were presented to the contingent on their departure. The field glasses were of very great benefit He remembered how once on looking through hie glasses he was able to see something wh;ch no one at the station knew anything a.: all ahoutt. That was the memorable action in which Colonel Gough was con_ corned. He saw the whole thing through his glasses, although it took place eighteen miles :sways. He saw the Begnehmen ganef) down and the Beers afterwards gallop down and take their position—a most interesting eight he could assure them. Unfortunately the force to which the Voluateers were attached was so extremely week and the poeition where the action took place was so far away that they could not seed help ;) Colonel Geugh. It would no doubt be interesting to some of those present te know how etre third contingent aetually spent their time ie South Africa. They left England ai.out the beginning of April and landed in Smith Africa at the end of that month. On landing at Cepetown they were out in open trunke and had re more or lees pleasant journey as far as Dundee. The journey occupied nine days and if anybody liked to travel nine days with , sixty three men in an open truck, all he could say was that taey were welcome to take his place. (Laughter.) He did not want to luxe aaything to de with it again. He had only one night in a truck hinaself,but he thought the other members of the contingent would echo his stetemeet. On the way up they had to land Private , Richardien at HeiCielburg where he was taken to hospital awl unfortunately died. After staving at Dundee a few days they set out for Da Jaager's drift. After being cooped up en board ship and in open trucks PO long, and considering the heat of the day, the length of the march, end the nature of the country, it was very creditable indeed that there was no falling out. They stayed at He Jaeger's drift for a month and during that time they wore engaged mostly in out-post work, making barbed wire entanglements and digging trenches, one of which was through solid rock. Lie should never forget the expressions of disgust which he overheard respecting the tools provided. Tee picks were about as sharp as the end of one's thumb and to out through solid reek these were not the best of tools to eineloy. After a month had elapsed they proceeded to Vryheid and were welcomed very heartily by the head- quarters of the South Lancashire Regiment at that time under the command of Lieutenant Colonel Adam. Lie- mediatetyon arrival they were shown a'pesition they had ; to take up in the outpost line and from the beginning of June to the middle of the following May they held that one position. The men were night in and night out, that was to say that omen night 4bey were on out- °let duty an i the next night they had a night in bed. They never moved from one particular kopje during the whole of that time and very monotonous it proved to some of noir numbers. Others seemed to appreciate it,in a mild way,but the majority did not seem to care for the jet). He might say that cen4idering the limited oppor- tunitiee which the men hel for enjoyment he really thought they made the best of it. He had the pleasure of living very close to them—about two yards away— for a Jeng time,end got t 3 knew hew they filled the time —l5-2, IS4, and a pair is 6—(laughter).— playing at cribbage. They had two cornete, a violin, and a " kerosine," an instrument which they had made themselves out of an old paraffin tin and telegraph sviee, and frern which they extracted music of a sort. (Laughter.) He was very gladto bear them tackling the monotony in the way they did. Re wished to thank the Mayor, Corporation, and tewnspeople for the very kind way in which they subscribed funds for the dependeate of soldiers at the frost, not only of the volunteers but of the men of various regiments from Warrington. One day whilst at the front he entered into cenversation with a private whom he had never met before, and asked him candidly what his feelings wore abeut his employment. The man replied " Well sir, as soon as I got to know that my wife and family were receiving some help from the Corporation of War- rington I was quite happy, and did not care how long I stopped out here,so long as I could do my little bit. The only thing thet upset me when I went out was as tee the wife and the kids." He also thanked those who had provided comforts for the men. Captain WILI,C)CIC3 responded on behalf of tholeurth section and said they esteemed the honoer more con- sidering that they had not had as much to do as the Volunteers who want out before them. When he Was going eat he said he had every confidence in the men he was taking out that they would be a credit to War- rington. His confidence had not been misplaced, for better men no-ene could wish to command!

Lieutenant RIDGWAY briefly responded and said he was !very pleased to get btck to Warrington. Es thought he should like ta go to South Africa but when he got there he did not think much of the placo. As thne went on he thought lois of it and now he had come to the conclution that there was no place like England and that Eogland was plenty large enough for him. (Laughter.) He was sure that a great many other who had been out were of the.same opinion.

RETURNED VOLUNTEERS - Dinner at the Warrington Town Hall.

Once more the Council;Chember7at the Town Hall was on Thursday Vast week the scene of a dinner to local active service volunteers. This time the guests were returned volunteers—the members of the third and fourth contingents supplied by the Ist Volunteer Battalion South Laacasbire Regiment. It will be remembered that the third contingent went out in April 1901 under the command of Captain B Fairclough. Unfortunately, one member of the de- tachment—Private A Richardson—succumbed to disease, but the other volunteers returned home safe and sound on June 25th of this year. The fourth contingent which was in charge of Captain Willcocks, left England on April 18th of this year. Owing to the conclu- sion of the peace negotiations the men had only a short spell of service at the Cape, and all arrived home on August Ist. At the dinner on Thursday evening opportunity was taken of presenting the volunteers with framed commemoration cards, similar to those which were received by the 211.961- bars of the first and second contingents, and swords of honour were presented to Captain Fairclough, Captain Willcocks, and Lieutenant Ridgway, son of the respected commanding officer of the 181 V.B. South Lancashire Regiment, who served at the front with the 2nd North Staff ordshire Regiment. The Mayor (Mir .T Charlton Parr) presided and amongst those present were the Rector (Rev Canon Willis), the Revs E J Willcocks and W Bracecamp,Alderman Fairclough, Councillors Bennett and Rogers,Colonel Ridgway, Major Rooms, Major Edelsten, Captains Hutchinson, rairclough, VVillcocka, and Appleton, Lieut. Ridgway Dr Pox, \lesiva R T Fairelough, II Thornton,J W Hayward, .1 Parkinson, Wolfenden, T Smith, W L Williams, Nelson Jackson, and J Lynn Whittle (town clerk). The Deputy-Mayor (Alderman Roberta) was unable to be present and wrote to the'Mayor 63 follows :—"Dear Mr Mayor, I am unexpectedly prevented from attending this Efvening:s celebration. Although not present with you, kindly allow me through the medium of this note to join with yourself and all our fellow towns• people in expressing our high appreciation of the patriotism displayed by, an 1 the services rendered to our empire by our own volunteer service sec- tions." Captain Carey who commanded the second contingent, had been invited to be present. but wired to the Town Clerk from Carlow :—" Please convey to the Mayor my great regret that I am un- avoidably prevented being present at dinner to- night." An excellent dinner was serval, the cater- ing of Mr Powys giving the greatest satisfaction. After dinner various toasts were honoured. The MAYOR °roomed the loyal toasts watch wore drunk with enthusiasm. cOUNCILLOR BENNE er AND RELIGIOUS DIFFERENCES.

Councillor BENNGYT proposed " The Bishops and Clergy and all ministers of religion." He said he thought that in this diocese they had the best of Bishops in Bishop Chavasse,and he noul I hardly express in words his high estimetion of the Bishop of LiverpooL go was sure they ;you'd all agree that in Warrington they had the best of Hectors, and he should run the risk of b sing fulsome if he said all that he thought of Canon Willis. He was also of the opinion that in Warringten they hal ! the bsst of clergy and ministers generally. They all ! tried to do their duty and were anxious to promote the ! moral and soirit xal well-being of the town, It had occurred to him that the bishops, clergy and ministers generally, not in Warrington only bet all over the world, might learn a valuable lesson from , their friends ire whose honour they had gathered that night—the soldiers. In the relent war the military forces of the empire all united to face a common foe and for the extension of a common kingdom. They were separated by all con- ceivable varieties of race and religion but when they had to ?ale the foe all thoir distinctions were forgotten and they al fought as soldiers of one king. It seemed to him that their friends the clergy and ministers might learn a leason from that. They were the servants of the one King and had to wage an even grander war against even greater enemies than those against whom the soldiers had been fighting so splendidly. Yet,nne ,s,rrieved to say that we saw them esnecially at the present time, divided amoug themselves.lnsteed of seeing them work- ing cordially and harmoniously together for the promotion of the greatest Empire of all—the Empire of the Prince of Peace—we foand them all wrangling and scinahbling, among themselves. In Warrington we had not seen a great deal of that. The clergy and ministers were too senaible, and, he believed, too Christian to indulge at any rate in any outward or particularly vivid signs of those minor differences. bathe thou:ht that throughout the country there was room for greater union among the V,IsTIOII9 ministers in faci of a common foe. The 'clergy and ministers had to fight net merely against ignorance, but against intemperance and immorality and even against war itself, for after all if the clergy and ministers absolutely fulfilled their duty they would be the greatest preachers of the doctrine of peace. They (his hearers) knew, however,that war was unavoidable under existing conditions and they knew how much soldiers owel to the way in which ministers had helped and encouraged them in the performance of their duty, but yet, the ultimate principle they were supposed to preach was the principle of peace on earth and goodwill to men. He should like to feel that in face of the common enemy the clergy and ministers dropped their minor differenoes and united to promote the great broad general principles on which they were agreed. Was it not possible that the Church of Eng- land, Roman Catholics, Weslevans, Congregationa- lists, and all the varions denominations might sink these minor matters and fight together for one King, one cause,and one Erneirenle did not want to asuro the role of clergy and ministers by preaching himself—(laughter) —but he thonght it was something of a scandal, In face of the terrible evils with which we had to contend that the clergy and ministers should not be able to present a more un'ted front to the common foe. The toast was heartily honoured.

In responding, the RECTOR, after congratulating the men, as chaplain to the Volunteers. upon their safe re- turn said be was united by very close ties to the offiners and their fathers, three of whom were present that night:: Be was greatly indebted to various members of the families for many kind acts and constant cc-operation in parochial work. Referring to Councillor Bennett's remarks, the R.ctor said the clergy belonged to the church militant and they had to fight for things that perhaps seemed minor points to some people, but were really points, as thev (the clergy) believed, of very great importance. But he believed that down in their hearts they had the very kindest feelings towards those who were supposed to differ from them and when occasion did arise ter them to unite and fight against one common enemy he hoped they would an be found shoulder to shoulder.

THE TOAST OF THE EVENING.

The MAYOU then proposed the tout of the evening— that of their gallant guests whom they were en pleased to we'come beck to their native country and to their native townelt gave them not only pleasure but a sense of satisfaction and relief—satisfaction that the men had been able to do the work that they volunteered to doland to bring back peace with honour, and relief that their anxiety was now over,and the men had returned safe and sound to resume those actuations and employments which they were obliged to intermit in consequence of theiropatriotiem in obeying duty's call. It was with very mixed feeling that they saw the volunteers go away—with feelings of pride and pleasure at their devotion and patriotism, at their public spirit, British pluck and bravery in responding to the call to arm, but it the same time with sadnEss in their hearts because they knew of the hardships, the dangers, and the risks which the men were bound to encounter. But now the Volunteers were beck again and they had only to rejoice They only missed, he believed, one of the fifty five who went out, and he unfortunately died in South Africa. They rejoiced that fifty four bad come back to resume their employment and to fight the battle of life at home instead of fighting it in South I Africa. It might require as much fortitude, determina- tion, and British pluck to fight the battle of life at home as it did abroad. He was pleased to hear that the Volunteers' employers in most cases had kept the men's places open, and he sincerely hoped they would be rewarded by the men falling back into their places and repaying their employers for the inconvenience they had suffered. He coupled with the toast the names of Captain Fairclougb, Captain Willcccks and Lieut.

As so many of the working class suffer from Indi- gestion, a letter received from Mrs Blake, of 49, R apart- street, Reading, cannot be too widely published. This lady writes : "I think it is only fair that everyone should know what a splendid thing Vi-Cocoa is as an appetiser and a refresher. It has no equal. I have been using it for the last three years, and have found it a splendid thing for indigestion, and I think if a few more of the working class would give Vi-Cocoa a trial they would greatly benefit by it and never use any other. Also to young mothers it is invaluable. I always recommend it wherever I go."

STOCKTON HEATH.

The revision for the parishes of Stockton Heath, Appleton, and Stratton was held at the Old London Bridge Inn on Tuesday afternoon, Mr Clement E Scott being the revising barrister. Mr John Ellison, was present as assistant overseer for lopteton and Stratton; Mr E Bradshaw for Stockton Heath; Mr W U J West- brook and Mr Dean on bailiff of the Wnservative party, and Mr Joseph Smith, of Kautsford, for the Liberal party. There were several new ownership olaims, one lodger claim, and one occupation claim, but there were no cases of public interest.

WARRINGTON DISTRICT NURSING ASSOUIATION. ---- Fifth Annual Report. ---- Proposed Purchase of the INurses' Home.

1 There was an encouraging attendance at the annual meeting of the Warrington District Nursing Association, held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey- street, on Monday evening. Mr Frederick dionks, J.P., who started the Association in Warrington, occupied the chair, and he 7743 accompanied on the platform by Miss Hughes, Inspector of County Nurses of London; the Rector (the Rev Canon Willis), the Revs E C E Carleton, and John Yonge ; Caunciilor John Roberts, and Messrs John Fair- clone, George Thompson, and J Duttou Fairclough (one of the hon. secretaries). THE CONIMINEE'S REPORP.

?dr J D FAtncLotrou read the fifth annual report as follows:—" The committee have much pleasure in submitting their report for the sth year's work, which continues to be very satisfactory. The thanks of the committee are duet. all the subscri- bers and the public holies whose contributions have made so welcome and substantial an addition to the funds. F)r details of work done the com- mittee refer subscribers and others to the super- intendent's report, which speaks for itself and indicates the number and variety of the eases visited. A reference to the treasurer's report will show the neces,ity for new and increasei financial support if the society is to continue a work com- mensurate with the needs of the town. During the year twelve ladies were elected as vics-presi- dents. To the superintendent and the nurses our warmest thanks are due for their untiring devotion to their duties and the efficient manner in which they are discharged." SUPEEUNCENDENT'S REPORT.

The report of Miss Whitfield (the superintendent) was as follows :—A general retrospect of the work of the Association during the past year shows great cause for thankfulness that so much bas been accomplished, in spite of drawbacks, and gives encouragement to go on to greater things in the future. The drawbacks have been chiefly owing to incompleteness of staff, one nurse havin7 resigned, great difficulty and delay were experienced in satis- factorily filling her place. It is extremely gratifying to note the increasing growth of interest, confid- ence and kindly feeling in the town generally, evidences of which have been more freely given than in any other year. Owing to the generosity of this Naylor and three friends, and to the kindness of Mrs Lewis, who cooks the food free of charge, the long-wished ,for 'lnvalid Kitchen " has been established. Our anticipations of its use- fulness have bean more than fulfilled, and by the supply of well-cooked food to patients whose sur- roundings and circumstances make elan the most elementary "cuisine " a matter of impossibility, many a convalescence has been hastened and many an illness has been shortened and rendered lass tedious Statistics :—Cases transferred from last year, 47; new cases, 451; visited once or twine but not entered, 56 ; visitepaid, 19,507. Result of cases nursed :—Convalescent or relieved, 286 ; transferred to hospital or Infirmary, 34 ; died, 89 ; removed trout books for other causes, ; remaining on book, Jane 30th, 1992, 34; total, 454. By wham sent in :—Medical men,253 ; medical men requested by patients or friends, 48; clergy,ministers,visitors, 35; patients or friends applied, 99 ; found by nurses, 14; 454. TREASURER'S REPORT.

The CHAIRMAN, in moving the adoption of the reports and accounts, stated that it had been suggested that the vacancy caused by the forthcoming departure from Warrington of the Rev F E Powell should ho filled hy Mr D Davies, chairman of the Cycle Parade Committee. (Hear, hear.) A letter had been received from the secre- tary of St. Mary's Dramatic Association intimating their intention to give a performance in aid of the funds of the Association. (Applause.) Since Gho accounts had been made up, he had received a letter from Mr Vera Davies stating that it was the intention of his brothers and himself toigive £5OO towards the purchase of the Nurses' Horne—(applause)—as a memorial to their late father and mother. They thought that would be far better than erecting a memorial window, and it would certainly be very useful. He (the Chairman) had also received £5O from Mr Thornton and £5 from Councillor Smethurst for the same object, and he was in hopes of getting a further donation of £250 from the Warrington Co-operative Society. (Applause). He knew the society was in possession of the money and he did not think they could make better use of it than by de- voting it to the purpose of purchasing a nurses' home. He had mdati pleasure in moving the adoption of the reports and in congratulating the nurses on the very ex- cellent work they were doing ; he looked upon it not as a work in opposition to the Infirmary but in conjunc- tion with it, He regarded their work as a Nursing

s Association as Infirmary work amongst the people,while at the Infirmary proper the nurses assisted those who , had mat with accidents or were suffering from serious diseases which required close personal medical atten- tion. There was a large amount of sicknese which could be assisted by the nurses throughout the town, and he heard it said on all sides that they were doiag a very useful work. Alluding to Miss Naylor's telegram with resoect to the invalid kitchen, the Chairman said that was a very important part of the work, alt Sough it was not the specifies work of the institution to find food. The names found it useful to be able to get properly cooked food in many cases of sickness, which they could not properly have if it were not for the assistaace of Mrs Lewis through this fund. He hoped the fund would be I supported so that Mrs Lewis would be able to give the assistance that was required in the coming year. With reference to the accounts of the Nursing Association, it would be noticed that considerable assistance lead been given by the Friendly Societiee. It was a new departure and he believed it wag due to the advocacy of a repre- sentative of the Druids Society wile paid a visit to the nurses home on the invitation of M 193 Weitfield. The money was very welcome and it would be seen that the aseoeeatien finished up the year with a balante ire hand of £ll 17s although they only started with a balance of n. 63 53. fie hoped the aesociatien woul I always be able to finish the year's working wish a balance is hand and not a balance against them. (Hear, hear). Toe Rev E C E CARLETON seconded, and :said there coull be no two opinions as to the value of the work of the Assoaistion when they considered that good nursing was often essential to effective medical treatment. I 7• wag impossible to dissociate it with what was dose by the medical profession ; and it had a claim on their support similar to that we'ch they were so willing to recegnize in the CiPO of the hospitals. The aim was of course the same as medical troatment,though necessarily subordinate to it. In the past there had not Seen a due recognition of the place that nursing ought to occupy. They had to thank Mr Monks for taking up the matter ix this lecslity and for placing mining upon a footing which was likely to be lasting. There was one thing to be kept in view. While pursu- ing their avocation as nurseslamonget the people they were in a position ta give practical hints and ieetruo- times in the art of nursing to those amongst whom they I worked. He supposed the time would never corns when suffering and sorrow and sickness would net have its claim ripen them as Christians,but the duty of r mend- ing to its call must keep pace with the urgency of the demand if they were to fulfil their calling as Christians, following in the footsteps of Him who ea earth made the sick and needy the objects of his special care and concern.

The Rev JOHN YoNGE supported, ael said there were Fe no very beautiful features about the work in connec- tion with the association, but One which especially pre- sented itself at a meeting of that character was that those who differed upon so many prolate were all one Linea this matter. (Hear. hear.) They were all called to ' take their share in alleviatiov human suffering and comforting human sorrow. The wonder to him was when he looked back upon the few years the association had been in existence atnoogst them how in the world they went on beforo they had it and how the poor suf- fering people got On without it. There wee no doubt whatever that the work ;which had been done by the institution was of the most valuable kind, and he as sure that it had a very warm place in the hearte of a great many people throughoet the length and breadth of the town. In the country as in Warringtm, the work of the district nurses was going on rapidly. Irs referring to the practical work of nurses, Miss :Hughes said a nurse should net only bring her best 'skill ta bear in her labo ire but learn to use that skill in the conditions under %Mich she foend the patient. In a hospital everething was arranged. T iere were venti- lation, warmth and various necessary appliances to add to the comfort of the sufferer, but in a workingrnan'S home, in many Oases, a different state of things existed. They must, therefore, train their nurses te remedy the existing defects and to give as little trouble and ieconvenieece as pessible to the people themselves. There must be no dictation but suggestions should be made in a kindly manner. Proceeding to refer to the objects of the Queen Victoria Jubilee Institute for nurses, Miss Hughes said they were two in number. The flrat was to properly train the nurses for their work and secondly, a regular insree- tion was in force to see that the work was going on on the right lines and that the standard of efficiency wag kept up. She must congratulate Warrington very warrnlY on its success. She had seen the home in Arpley-stree,t and she feend it to be most cernFortable and suitable is every way for the nurses. She had seen homes that had been specially befit for the purpoee, but she had seen none which were more coovenient than the War- rington home. It had been a great pleasure to come to the meeting and hear that the work of the nurses was appreciated and that the work was going on so well. (Applause,)

When Frederic' Prince of Wales, father of George ill., died, a fashieir able minister preached a sermon on that event in Mar fair Chapel, London, in the course of which he re; marked : "The Prince had no great parts, but he great virtues ; indeed, they degenerated into vices : b; was very generous, but I hear his generosity has ruicse° a great many people : and then his condescension was/ such that he kept very bad company." In similar style it is constantly said of some allege! remedial agents that they are well known. True; for what are they well known? For their remedit power? Not always. WARRINGTON.

A Carter's Fatal Fall.

At the Coroner's Court on Monday evening, Mr Brighouse conducted an inquiry relative to the death of Richard Rowlinson, 67 years of age, a carter formerly in the employ of Mr Isherwood,con- tractor.—Mr Unsworth appeared on behalf of Mr Isherwood and Mr Longton for the relatives.—Mrs Huzzey, a widow, living .at 50, Pierpoint-street, stated that the deceased had lodged with her for the past twelve months. On the 14th of June last he came home and said he bad hurt his left side by falling off his lurry at Messrs Crosfield's works whilst loading there. She poulticed him and after- wards went for Dr Joseph who attended him. He appeared to get better but on the 13th inst he became worse and died on the 25th inst. She asked the doctor what was the matter with deceased and he told her that deceased had fractured the lower rib on the left side and had lacerated the kidneys—William Johnson, crane driver at Messrs Crosfield's works,residing at 14, Milner-street,stated that about two o'clock en Saturday, the 14th June last, be was assisting the deceased to load his Itirry with cases of glycerine, each of which weighed about 11 cwts. The deceased was on the lurry and was in the act of placing one of the elves in position when he slipped and fell off the vehicle on to an iron drum which was on the side of the lurry. De- ceased did not appear to be much injured at the time and was able to take his horse and lurry away from the yard.—Richard Rowlinson, eldest son of the deceased, stated that he saw his father re- peatedly afer the accident. His father told him how the accident occured, but he never said any- thing which would lead witness to suppose anyone was to blame for the accident.—The jury returned a verdict of" Accidental Death."

===== 0002601_19021003_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
Coming of Age Celebration.

Last Friday evening about 30 of the workmen of Messrs G Artingetall and Co„ wire-weavers, metal perforators and brush manufacturers, Latchford Works, assembled at the Wiumarleigh Cafe, Sankey- street, to celebrate the coming of age of Mr William O'Brien, of the Engineering Department. After an excellent and substantial dinner, for which expres- sions of appreciation were duly tendered to the caterers, the company settled themselves for a social evening, presided ever by Mr Arthur Griffiths I (general foreman), supported by Mr P O'Brien, Mr Daniel Jones, Mr W A Jones, &c. The vice-chair was occupied by Mr W Miller. The toasts of " The King," "The Queen and Royal Family," having been duly honoured, Mr A Griffiths delivered a speech, pointing out bow under varying conditions the majority of those present had been associated with Mr W O'Brien (for the past seven years), the high appreciation in which they held his conduct character and efforts, and his sincere hope' that his life and action in the future would tend to intensify rather than diminish their regard and respectiThese remarks were supplemented in a capital speech by Mr W Miller,who as an older but contemporary apprentice and fellow workman, wished him health and future prosperity. This toast was cordially accepted, and in response Mr W O'Brien thanked all his friends and fellow work- men, absent and present for the kindly goodwill and cheerful words he had ever received from them during the period of his apprenticeship, making it a time to be looked back'upon with pleasure. Mr P O'Brien proposed "The future prosperity of Messrs 0 Artingstall and Co," coupling with it the health and happiness of the Managing Director, Mr Arthur Jones, who though' unable to be present with them through pressure of other engagements, had given expression of his sympathy with the gathering. This toast was enthusiastically received with musical honours, and responded to by Mr :Daniel Jones and Mr W A Jones who expressed their deep sense of gratification and pleasure at the cordial and kindly reception of their father's name. During the evening the fol- lowing programme was carried out :—Songs, Mr A Griffiths, " Mona,"" Jacks come home to-day "and "Johnny Sands " ; songs, Mr Houghton, " Mary of Argyle "and "'There is a flower that bloometh song, Mr Howard, "The Banks of Allan Water"; recitation, Mr Matthewman, " firing butter "; song, Mr Wright, "The Fighting Fusiliers " ; song, Mr T Hockenbull," Genevieve"; song, Mr P O'Brien,"The Southron Sea "; Mr Bert Wright ably fulfilling the duties of pianist. The health of the chairman being toasted, the singing of the "National Anthem ' brought a very pleasant evening to a conclusion.

THE LATE MR. ELIJAH FORD.

The Funeral.

On Saturday afternoon the remains of the late Mr Elijah Ford, whose painfully sudden death was reported in our last issue, were interred in the Warrington Ceme- tery. The first part of the funeral service was conduc- ted in Bold-street Wesleyan Church in the presence of relatives and friends of the deceased dnd many local preachers. The ministers present were the Revs G H Carnson, W C J Neville, and J. Gunnell. The Rev G H Carnson, the newly-appoin- ted superintendent of the circuit, said he had not had the pleasure of knowing the deceased, but what he bad heard of him was to his credit and glory. It was not given to every man to be a local preacher for 44 years. That was quite a long stretch, and a record of goad and useful toil in connection with the Church of Christ. It was a source of great joy to the family to know that Mr Ford's Church work was acceptable throughout the whole of that time, and that people desired to listen to him. He had heard that their brathcr was a good class leader. Let them hope that that portion of Church work was not falling away. He knew he had been out of health for some time and the family had been anxious about him, but he doubted whether they were prepared for such a sudden end. Sadden death—sudden glory—was an old Methodist phrase and, if all was right with one, it was a true phrase. Prayer having been offered, the organist played the dead march in" Saul." The cortege then proceeded to the cemetery, the hearse being preceded by the follow- ing :—Roos G H Carnson, W C J Neville, and J Gunnell (Wesley an ministers), Messrs S Moss, W C Nicholson, I Johnson, P Stringer, W Collier, F Wright, W Light, J Bailey, M Osborne, S B Stringer, J II Wildman, A Perkins, Ackerley, E W Evans, G W Littler, II Waldman and R Moss (local preachers) ; and Messrs F W Pearson, J Ditchfield. F Dickson, W Gleave and _G Word, and T Heaver. The mourners were :—Messrs W H Fcrd, A Ford. and Masters Percy and John Ford (Sons), Mrs Joseph Ford, Misses Nellie and Ethel Ford (daughters), Mrs Howarth,MancheEter, and Mrs Roberts, Weaverham (sisters) ; Mrs Joseph Ford (son-in-law) ; Mr W H Ford, Manchester (nephew) and Councillor

At the graveside several other friends were present in- cluding Councillor A Bennett, and Messrs J W Hayward, A Lunt, R Lunt, T S Steel, Richardson, W Bennett, C Roylance (Moore), R Cook, E Lawson, and R Lewis. The service was conducted by the Revs G il Carnson and WC J Neville in a very impressive manner. Many floral tributes had been received including one from the Moore Wesleyan Church "as a mark of esteem and respect."

HILL CLIFFE BAPTIST CHAPEL.

The autumnal anniversary in connection with the above church was held last Sunday—somewhat later than' the usual time—but fortunately the weather, so important a factor in the suceess of these services. proved very favourable and ona of the finest Sundays of the few enjoyed this last summer was experienced. As a Jesuit the services, all day were very successful. In the morning a chi.dren's service was held and there was a gocd attendance of scholare, a large number of whom brought contributions of fl ewers which were placed in the Comunion by the teachers and presented a beauti- ful and refreshing sight, throughout the day. The preacher was the Rev Thomas Ho', heirs°, the blind pastor of Middleton, who gave a very suitable and practical address on " Children's Crumbs "and he was listened to by the children with special interest. Suitable hymns were sung by the children and a pleasing service was enjoyed.—ln the afternoon the chapel was well filled with an adult congregation, and the Rev Thomas Holehouse again preached on " He death all things well," an effective and touching exposition of the dealings of God in providence. The choir sang the anthem " Praise the Lord 0 Jerusalem " in good time and tune.—Tea was provided in the sehool- room for friends at a distance, and any others who wished to stay at the evening service, and a considerable number availed themselves of the provision; spending the interval between tea and service in walking through ' the graveyard, which had been thrown open for the occasion, and the lanes round about, greatly enjoying the autumn scene.—ln the evening the chapel was full twenty minutes before time of service, and when toe service began was packed to overflowing, includiag the adjoining schoolroom. Such a crowded congregation has rarely if ever assembled before on these autumnal oceasione. The Rev Thomas Holehouse again occupied the pulpit,accompanied at each service her the pastor, the Rev J S Hughes, but it is interesting to state that blind though Mr Holehouse is, and that from his earliest in- I fancy, he conducted the entire service, giving out the hymns and reading the lessons, as any other preacher would, Mr Hughes g.ving nut the announcements only. Mr Holehouse's topic at night was "The beauty of the Lord "and he preached an excellent sermon, full of beautiful thou:hes and pathetic appeal,and was liitened to with rapt attention. The choir gave a capital render- ing of the anthem " God reigneth." Mr Willie Hellas, the organist, presided at the organ all day, and played with great ability euch voluntaries as "Ave Verum," communion in A flat, " Offertoire," " Trumpet March," and " 0 Thou that hearest prayer " and others. The collections for the day amounted to close upon £l4, exceeding those of last year. The flowers were distributed after the close ef the evening service for sick ones at home and the Warrington lafirmary.
