FRIENDLY SOCIETY PRESENTATION TO MR. S. TAYLOR.

At a concert arranged by the Runcorn friendly societies and held in the Tcchnical Institute on Monday, Mr S Taylor,chairman of the Urban District Council was pvesented with a handsome illuminated address from the members of the Duke's Club Friendly Society, the "RoE e of Sharon" Lodge (No. 1,195) G.U.O. Oddfellows, and the" Lily of the Vailey" Lodge (No. 515 G.U.O. Odd- fellows. The address, which was a specimen of elegant penmanstrip,and handsomely framed, read as follows : •• Coronation of B.M. King Edward VII., August 9:h, 1902. To Samuel Taylor, Eq., J.P., chairman of the Runcorn Urban District Council. Sir,—Your invita- tion to the Runcorn friendly societies to take part is the public celebration of the Coronation of the King Cords a favourable opportunity to express our respect and regard for you as a townsman who for a long ani continwus period has taken an onerous and honourable share in all the duties and obligations of a citizen. You have on many ock:asions, both in public sad pri7ate,shown a cordial interest in our local institu- tions,especially those having as their object the welfare of the industrial section of the community. Your con- stant advocacy of the necessity for an accident hosoital as a worthy commemoration of the Coronation of King Edward the Seventh,your munificent support and active work in its behalf, and its erection during your chair- manship, for the fourth time, of our local governing body, abundantly prove your sympathy with the kind of suffering to which the members of our societies are unfortunately always exposed, and your desire to supplement the work of our societies in a most practical and helpful form. We trust that you may have vouchsafed to your life, health and oppor- tunity to continue your encouraging interest in all movements,ior the progress of our town and the well- being of its inhabitants.—On behalf of the Duke's Club Friendly Society, Robert William Owen, James Hankers George HoUngworth ; Rose of Sharon ' Lodge, No. 1,195, G.U.O. They hoped the building would be opened in the early part of January, and that it would be opened free from debt. (Applause )

HEAVY' FINE FOR POACHING. AT NEWTON.

- At the Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday, Robert Jones and Jaosea Hornby,of Pemberton,were charged on two informations with coming from land in the occupa- tion of Mr Robert Stone, Newton, and being in possession of a number of rabbits aud poaching implements. Jones did not appear, and Mernby pleaded guilty.—P S Bancks stated that at 4 45 a m on September 24th he was in Park Side-road. Newton, in company with P C Benson and two other con- stables, wen he saw the defendants coming along the road each carrying a bag. lie told them he suspected they were unlawfully in possession of game. Jones said : think you have been watching us all night." while Nornby remarked : "it is the first time for me." Witness then searched them. Jones's bag •contained Ems rabbits, nine pegs, a long net, and a stone. and Hornby had a long line, suet. seven pegs, a stone and ,three rabbits. Witness took possession of them.—Ques- tioned by Inspector Forster witness said the length of the nets was 53 yards. The line was known as a driving line, with which to beat the fields, instead of em- NlOying dogs Stcnes were often .used as weanms. Witness had, had them used to him before.—ln- spector Forster said he tad had complaints from gentlemen who owned shooting land in the neighbourhood where defen- dants were found. Defendants belonged to a gang of poanh- re, Jones having been previously convicted for a similar offence. —Jones was fined 20s and costs in each case, and Horaby I.os .and costs on each tharge, the nets and lines to be forfeited.

CAIRO-STREET UNITARIAN CHAPEL, WARRINGTON.

The annual harvest festival in connection with this place of worship was held on Sunday. There were large congregations, especially in the evening, when chairs had to he placed in the ahlas for the accommodation of worshippers. The decorations, though not on an exten- sive scale were pretty arid effective, and were much ad- mired. The musical part of the services was of a very attractive character. A number of suitable hymns were sung, a Lid a couple of anthems rendered in a highly creditable manner by the choir, namely, in the morning "Look on the fields" (C. Macpherson); and evening " Behold I give you every herb" (Cuthbert Harris). Mr J M Muir presided at the organ. In the, afternoon a special school service was held in the chapel, and was conducted by Mr H S Knowles. The preacher, morning and evening, am the Rev Charles Hargrove. M.A., of Leeds. His discourse in the. E vening was based on the nVents recorded in the 16th chapter of Exodus—including the supply of bread from heaven—and also the feeding of 5,000 men with five barley loaves and two fishes. The preacher contended that even these miracles were as nothing compared with the feeding of the people of the world with bread year after year. There was the bare field to be turned up by the plough—a most unpro- mising looking field compared with what it was a short time before. The seed of which the supply was so small compared with the wcrld'a demand was scattered broad- cast. What magic charm could recover it again? What scientific invention could be found to meet tem case ? None at all. The miracle that now took place was greater than any recorded in the Bible. Though they bad no control over these things, they trusted God. He was not fickle that He should give and take away. tie neither slumbered nor slept, and it was their duty to trust Him to the utmost and always.—The offertories were on behalf of the District Nursing Association.

ST. MARY'S, GREAT SANKEY.

A great event in the yearly routine of the church work is the harvest thanksgiving service and although the preparation of the church involves a great amount of hard work, the time is always ungrudgingly given by the lady members of the congregation. It is ques- tionable if any church in the district can compare with the Sankey Parish Church for lavish and tasteful decora- tion on Harvest Thanksgiving Day. Certain it is that at the evening service the Church is unfailingly packed by visitors from other parishes and every available inch of space occupied. The farmers and others in the parish have as usuahgiven of their corn and produce to decorate the ledifice, and the opinion was freely expressed on Sunday that the effect was even better than usual. At the ' approach to the chancel was erected a screen of corn , and flowers, while the pillars and gas standards were wreathed with serpentine strings of corn &o. The win- dow bottoms were decorated with splendid fruit and flowers, and suitable mottoes, such as " Holy is the Harvest," and "Thou crow nest the year with Thy good- ness," were effectively displayed. Some very fine I baskets of ferns were suspended from the I principals, and the pulpit and font were tastefully embellished. The morning service was I conducted by the Vicar, the Rev. J. R. Jones M.A., and was very well attended. He preached a telling ser- mon from the 10th and 11th verses of the 23rd chapter of Leviticus. The choir gave a very finc rendering of the anthem, " Great is the Lord," and harvest hymns were sung. In the afternoon a children's flower 82rvice was held, when the Vicar delivered a suitable address, , and pointed out the lessons of the harvest to the children At the conclusion of the service the children brought their offerings of flowers and laid them on the chancel table. These were afterwards sent to the Infirmary. The evening service was a crowded one, and the clergy were the Rev J R Jones and Rev Alden Nash, of Liver- pool. The hymns were specially selected,and the anthem I "And God said" was well rendered by the choir. Preaching from the 26th verse of the 34th chapter of I Exodus, the Rev Alden Nash gave a picturesque account I of the ancient harvest festival. He was attentively listened to throughout. Collections were taken et each service and realized the sum of £27 14s. Miss M E Standish officiated at the organ at each service.

PEARSON AND KNOWLES'COAL AND IRON CO, LIMITED. 29th General Meeting.

A Successful Year.

The 29th ordinary general meeting of rho above com- pany was held at the company's offioes, Warrington, on Tuesday, " for the purpose of ree3iving and passing the accounts for the year ending June 30th, and for transacting the general business of the company." Mr J S Harmood Banner (chairman of the Board of Directors), presided, and the other directors present were Mr William H Sleekly (deputy ebalrman), Mr Ed. Beck, Mr John J Bleckly, Mr George F Pearson, Mr Rd Brancker,and Mr W Peter Rylands ; shareholders Messrs II Sleekly, John Knowles, J A Littltr, Vim Richardson, J Paul Rvlands, David Garnett, Thomas Gandy, jun., Ctias E Bunch, John Sleekly, J and A S Sleekly, with Mr James Dodds (secretary of the company) Mr W 8 Blease (one of the auditors).

===== 0002601_19021003_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
AUDITOR'S REPORT.

The SECRETARY having read the notice convening the meeting and the auditors' report, the Chairman pro- duced a list of the shareholders,and said that duly sealed proxies bad been received representing 265 shareholders and 11,946 share!.

THE REPORT OF THE DIRECTORS.

The report of the directors is as follows :—" The profits for the year ending June 30th, 1902, amount to £62,864 13s 103, and the balance brought forward tram last year is £10,133 85, together £72,993 is 103. An interim dividend at the rate of 6 per cent per annum on both preference and ordinary shares was paid in March last for the half-rear ending December 31st, 1901, amounting to £25,362 105, and the directors now recom- mend that for the past half-year a similar dividend be declared, making a total distribution of £50,709 7s 63 for the year. The directors further recommend that £5,000 be written off the colliery leases and goodwill account, and £lO,OOO be added to the reserve fund, and that the balance of £7,288 14s 43 be carried farward. With the object of safe-guarding and developing the company's trade in wire rode, which constitutes one of the most important branches of their business,the direc- tors have invested a part of the reserve fund in the acquisition of shares, giving a preponderating interest in the works of their largest customer, with whom they have been in close alliance from the earliest days of the company's existence, and by agreement with the execu- tors of the late proprietors the whole property will gradually be taken over. The course of business during the year has been unsatisfactory, and both coal and iron have suffered from declining prices and curtailed profits' largely due to the severity of foreign competi- tion and the dislocation of trade brought stout by the war in South Africa. In compliance with the articles of association, Mr W Peter Rylands retires from the Board, and is eligible for re-election. The auditors, Messrs Blease and Sons, also retire, and are eligible for re-election.—(Signed) J• S. HARMOOD BANNER, chair- man." Mr BANNER,hI moving the adoption of the reports con- gratulated the shareholders upon the result af the year's working, at any rate in reference to the coal trade. The balance sheet was carefully analysed, and the Chairman remarked that between June, 1900, and June this year the price of coal had decreased from 133 33 to 83, and wages had declined only 83 a ton. Colliery proprietors had, therefore, no ordinary difficulties to face. Fortun- ately this company had not paid away their profits in dividends up to the hilt in the past. (Hear,hear.) They had built up such a reserve fund—and it would be seen from the balance sheet that they were making a sub- stantial addition to it that day—that they were in a position to face difficult times with more courage than would otherwise have been the case. He hoped the combination which had been recently effected in the iron department would assure them of a fair average profit, but it was very difficult to compete against foreigners who, when the slightest demand arose, came to this country and " dumped " down their stuff hers at prices below the cost of manufacture. They had also to contend against the fact that Americans had recently raised a great demand for iron in this country, which had resulted in an advance in the price of raw material. Mn. R. H. BLECKLY AND FOREIGN COMPETITION.

_ Mr. W. U. BLECICLY, in secoadicg the motion, said : —I wish once more to draw your attention for a moment to the serious question of foreign competition, which bids fair in the near future to destroy a large por- tion of the iron and steel export trade of this country, not because we cannot beat the foreigners on even terms, for we can, but because we are pitted against matiufacturers who by reason of large protective tariffs at home, are enabled to sell to our customers abroad materially below the cost of production either in their own country or ours. I told you last year that one article which we produce to the extent of 25,000 tons per annum our competitors in Germany were se ling for exportation at a loss of upwards of £2 per ton, which their enormous tariff enabled them to do. This still goes on. One of our _ _

customers, the head of a large firm of merchants, wrote us a short time ag3. in reference to our foreign corn- pctitorte prices, winding up by saying: "Our manu- facturers want waking up." He absolutely thought that by waking up we could meet this kind of competi- tion, and make a profit when Germans were prepared to sell at a loss of more than £2 aton, if necessary, to take our trade from US. I decline to admit that we are not awake. We are always glad, willing, and anxious to learn, and we even flatter ourselves that at the present moment there are many things we can manufacture better and cheaper than the foreigners. (Hear, hear). But what trade can compete eventually against foreign bounties such as we have to meet ? The old advice was "find new markets." Unfortunately there are not many new markets left in the world, and as soon as they are found our bounty-fed competitors are in a better position to supply them than we are. South Africa promises to be a good market, but, like India, I am afraid the bulk of its iron and steel require- ments will be supplied by the foreigner as long as the present high tariffs prevail. It is not for British manufacturers to wake up—they are awake already—but the proper people to wake up are the British shareholders who are being deprived of their dividends' and British working men , who are being robbed of their living by having to work short time, when but for the foreign tariffs there would be good dividends for the one and work enough to keep the others working full time. This is a digression, but I think it is very necessary that we ought all to under- stand the difficulties that this foreign competition is placicg us in. We may work as economically as we like we may look after our own markets, we may meet our customers in every possible way, as it is our study always to do: We are wide enough awake to look after that sort of thing, but however wide awake we are we cannot alter the question c,f the tariff, which is killing our trade by inches. I have great pleasure in sr cond- ing the resolution that the report now submitted be approved and adopted. The report was then unanimously passed. The CHAIRMAN moved : " That the usual dividend at the rate of 6 per cent, per annum on the preference shares, for the half year ending the 301,13 June last, and a dividend at the rate of 6 per coat, per annum, free of income tax, on the ordinary shares, be paid on and after this date." Mr J J BLECRLY, ih seconding, said : I think the general conclusion we must arrive at,after hearing what we have heard this morning, is thaa our, trade is in a very difficult position. We can only console ourselves with the fact that we are sailing in a well-found vessel, well manned with r o3d officers. and that we mean to fight it through. (Hear, bear.) If the iron trade of this country is doomed we must submit, but as long as there is an iron trade to carry on we mean to have our share In it. I have much pleasure in seconding the resolu- tion.

The resolution having been passed. We regret to learn that the Americana or the Germans can show any superiority to U 9 in res- pect of the internal management of their business. We have heard the statement of the financial side very clearly put,and I wish to endorse what Mr John Sleekly has said, that we are not crying out in tones of despair. The Board of this company feel that it is their duty to point out the dangers of the time and to do what they ran to assist in any movement that will counteract the difficulties Under which the country is labouring. There is not the slightest feeling, lam sure, in thi country to sit down and give up the fight, for we ar all determined to fight, and if it is a case of " backs to the wall," " backs to the wall "it will be. We are net going to cry out and say we cannot h)ld our own until we are compelled to. The CHAIRMAN : In putting that resolution, I can only say I heartily endorse those remarks.

SIR C. W. DILKE, M.P., AT WARRINGI ON.

Conference of Trade Unionists.

Sir Charles W. Dilke, M.P, visited Warrington on Wednesday evening to speak at a conference of local trade unionista, called for the purpose of discussing the Shops Bill introduced into Parliament by Sir Charles, and the question of labour representation. The conference Was held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, and was attended by representatives ' of the various trade union branches- The pro- ceedings were private, admission being by ticket. Mr John Edwardson (president of the Trades and Labour Council) presided, and in the course of his introductory remarks spoke of the ' necessity for improvement in the conditions under which shop assistants worked. He also referred to the advisability of trade unionists being represented inParliament and:en local governing bodies. Recent- ly a resolution was brought before the Warrington Town Council that in case of all disputes the men should have their official representative i to save the men making scapegoats of themselves. A reso- lution was adopted,but not the one drawn up by the Trades Council. Speaking of the Taff Vale decision the Chairman said it was hard when a man had contributed his savings to a trade union to provide for himself in case of sickness or dispute to have that money taken away as the result of some paltry dispute. Mr J A SEDDON, of St. Helens (Presi- dent of the Shop Assistaats' Union) pro- posed the following resolution : " Tha t this meeting condemns the unreasonably long hours worked in shops, whereby the social and moral well-being of the assistants is seriously affected, their physical and intellectual develop- ment retarded, and welcomes the introduction of the Shops Bill in the House of Commons, by Sir Charles W Dilke, as a reasonable and practical measure for the protection of the shop workers of the country. It also pledges itself to further the progress ot the Bill by every means that lies in its power." At the outset he expressed his thanks to Sir Charles Duke for all he had done on behalf of shop assistants. Sir Charles took up the shop assistants' question when the shop assist- ants themselves hardly realized the difficulties under which they laboured. Under his leadership they had now reached Pisgah's Mount and they saw the promised !and. There was however, much ground to be covered and the shop assistants needed more than the assistance of members of their own trade. That night he asked the workingmen of Warrington to ;give them not only their assent to that resolution, but to assist them by their votes in municipal and Parliamentary elections. The shop Bill was extremely moderate. It asked that the assistants should work sixty hours per week. Per- haps that would be somewhat of a surprise to some trade unionists who were working fifty-two hours and claiming a reduction to forty-eight. It also asked that those sixty hours should not be in the hands of the employer only to regulate as he thought fit. There were employers in this country who looked upon their employees as so many pieces of machinery to fit into the machine. The Bill laid down that only so many hours should be worked each day, and the sum total would be sixty hours per week. It also gave the local authority the option of saying on which days the shops should be closed earlier. Employers would be allowed to work their employees overtime on twenty-four days in the year for three hours per day. Shop assist- ants were not paid for overtime, so they would be giving their employers a very good week's work for nothing. The Bill made provisien, and rightly too, for Sunday closing. Sunday opening was becoming a growing evil in the large towns, and shops in Liverpool that opened on Sunday morning and well on in the after- noon could be counted by the thousand. Shama). The Bill also provided that the ages of boys or girls entering the distributive trade should not be under fourteen years. His (the speaker's) experience was that under the present conditions boys of nine, ten, and eleven years of age bad been drafted off at eleven and twelve o'clock on Saturday night and even in the small hours of Sunday morning with parcels almost as biz as themselves to deliver to the inconsiderate British public. The speaker pointed El t :,centresiof distribution dealing solely in medicine, medical or surgical appliances, refreshments for consumption on the premises, tobacco, pipes, matches, and smoker's requisites, newspapers, fresh fruit, and intoxicating liquors were to be exempted from the operation of the Bill. He a.ked his teasers as trade unionists, and men who looked out for these creature comforts, what fault they could find with the Bill. He did not think the Bill .demanded anything but what was fair and just and right. Mr J FARGIHER, president of the Warringtan Branch of the Shop Assistants Union, seconded. He believed that there was no remedy to the present conditions except by the Bill. Voluntary effort had been tried and had proved a failure. They had laboured in Warrington tor , the last ten or twelve years tiseiffnatrhe_vfoliitir effortunta;yfrob-g ainna. what had they got for their ning to end. If they went along the principal striets , to-day they would find a shop here and a shop thero open long after the general hour of closing. Did not that prove that local effort was a failure? Sir Charles W DILKE, M.P., who was very cordiall , received, said his visit co Warrington and that cmfet- ence were the result of a suegestioa by the Wartiagton Trades Council. They were good enoogh to ask him to come to Warrington during the last Par- liamentary Session when he could not break his rule of never engrgiug himself to be absent from the sittings of ; the House of Common!. At that time he promised them , that if he could see his way to come later on he would j do so. There was, however, such a short time at his disposal for going about the country before the resump, , tion of Parliament, that when Conferences were being decided upon by himself in conneation with the j National Union of Shops Assistants, he suggested that , they should kill two birds with one stone, and ask the Trades Council to allow him to address their monitors and the shop assistants at a j dot conference. That was the fourth of such conferences which had recently taken place, and some six or eight were going to take place with the next fortnight. Those wnich had already , been held had been very useful, and judging by the j commencement of their proceedings that night he thought they would be able to say the same of that gathering. The resolution itself began with a st itensent as to the evils of the present wrong hours which did not need any proving. It mast be taken as absolutely ad- mitted on all sides. The recent House of Lords Com- mittee on working hours unanimously found these facts to exist in almost the exact words which were made use , of in that resolution. It Was a curious fact, however, that these evils had beau admitted for an Immensely long time, and yet really nothing had been done legislatively and not very much otherwise in some places towards removing them. He said in some places because there was an extraordins ary difference between town and town. In some parts of the country there was not very much I o object to In the hours. They were very much the hours that they would propose in the Bill, but in some parts of the °lunar, these rlasonable hours were extraordinarily exceeded Although they had made no progress yet in legislation, they had, he thoul4ht, mhde great progress in public opinion—great progress in increasing the numbers of those who thought that these evils which had to be dealt with by law and on the general lines oftthe shop Bill proposal. They had load an extraordinary conversion—they had converted Lord Salisbury. Of all the difficult peopl3 to convert in the whole country he simeH think Lord S aliabur y was the most difficult. Lord Salisbury was t first absolutely oppcsed to legislation on this subject, A committee was, however, obtained on behalf of the rival, and they (the shop assistants) thought the less satisfactory measure—Sir John Lubbock's Bill. The committee was rather intended he (the speaker) thought by its con- stitution to pass a unanimous resolution in favour of tbat measure, but it did net, and the reason was that Lord Salisbury, who was a member of .the committee, came there on the last day and upset the report of the committee and made them unanimously swallow report of his own. (Laughter.) That report was not in thn direction of no legislation ; on the contrary it was in the direction of legislaticn and of their promoters ethe of the I present Bill) legislation. That:he called a most extra- I ordinary nonversion.SirCharles went cti to point out how difficult it was to carry a Bill upon the subjeet. No private member, in the present state of the forms of the House of Commons could carry a really opposed Bill on 1

any subject. That was to say that if a Bill was strongly I opposed by even as few as two members at every stage of its eerie Once it could not pass in the present state of the forms of the Rouse of Commons. After referring at length to the difficulties of Parliamentary procedure Sir Charles said it was easier fer them by organized effort to obtain time for resolutions than for Bills. They were obliged, of course, to hand themselves together for that purpose. There was a very small group of members in the House who acted together as labour members and it was necessary to co-operate with others in order to obtains time for resolutions. Lately they had had con- ference of all those members who were interested in labour legislalation and they had tried to procure co-operation for the purpose of moving forward resolu- tions which they had agreed upon. He thought they would try to withdraw the Bill, as would be necessary after a day or two, to move the resolution, which would be to the effect that in the opinion of the Huse the unnecessarily long hours of labour in shops were injurious to tae well-being of all concerned, and that it was deeirable that borough and district Councils should b 3 authorized to obtain provisional orders to make suoh regulations in respect to the closing of shoes and the limitation of the hours of labour as might seem neceseary for the areas under their jurisdiction. ALLEGED UNLAWFUL WOUNDING AT NEW 7 ON.

Woman Committed for Trial.

At the Warrington Occasional Court on Thursday before Captain Withington, Alma Blanche Kelly was charged with unlawfully wounding Maria Kenny, at Hazel-grove, Newton. on the 19th of Sep- bar, by striking her with a glass bottle.—Prisoner pleaded not guilty. Prosecutrix said she had been across the back street at about midnight on the date in question. Mrs Kelly came up and struck her twice on the heal with a bottle which she had been carrying behind her back. 'Witness fell down, and her head bled very much. The prisoner then ran up the street and witness was taken to Dr Valentine's surgery. She had not spoken to Mrs Kelly. In reply to the prisoner, she denied having called her foul names. When the prisoner went away witness's husband came up and hit her with a crutch on the head.

Mrs Carlin corroborated prosecutrix's evidence. Dr William Valentine said the prosecutrix was brought to his surgery at one o'clock on the morn- ing of September 20th. He found a lacerated and contused wound,three inches long on the right side of the head, which extended to the bone. A bottle of the character of the pieces of the glass produced would cause the wound.

By the prisoner: The wound might have been caused by a crutch, but he did not think so. _ _ _ _

P.O Mellorotatio'ned at Earlestown, said he found the pieces of broken bottle produced. There was blood on the ground about a foot away from the glass. He arrested Mrs Kelly at two o'clock in the morning. He charged her, and she replied : "No it was not with the bottle." Prisoner said she never struck the complainant. It was her husband who had hit her with a crutch.

Mrs Carson. a neighbour, was called by Mrs Kenny. She said at midnight on the 19th Sep- tember. she was going into her own house, and saw Mrs Kenny come with a poker in her hand and try to break her window. She threw it through the window and broke a bottle. She never saw Mrs Kelly go near to the prosecutrix, and she did not nave a bottle. In consequence of Mrs Kenny throwing the poker through her window, witness threw some dirty water at her. Mrs Kenny's hus- band then came out, and in trying to hit witness he struck his wife on the forehead with the crutch. The prisoner was not there at the time. Mrs Kenny was bleeding and shouted for help. The pieces of bottle produced were part of a vinegar bottle which was inside her house, and had been broken. She threw the pieces out of the window. Prisoner was committed for trial at the next Liverpool Assizes, bail being allowed in two sureties of £2O each.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLES FOWN.

BY A CORRESPONDEN T] .

There was plenty of excitement on the Earles- town football ground on Saturday afternoon, when Stalybridge Rovers and Earlestown contended for the right to enter the real competition for the Lancashire Cup. There was a large gate and the keenest interest was taken in the match. Like all other cup competitions, too much acrimony entered imo the game, with the consequence that several men were injured. " Andy " Limond was badly I kicked and it is quite likely he will not be able to play again for some weeks. With only ten men Earlestown were somewhat handicapped,and Staly- bridge managed to open the scoring. It was fortun- ate for them that they succeeded in obtaining a goal when playing down bill, for when they changed over, Earlestown hustled them to such an extent that they were unable, except on one or two occasions to get out of their own goal and even then they were never dangerous. Earlestown suffered very hard luck, but when they equalised, they simply swarmed round their opponents goal, the Stalvbridge custodian having an extremely lively time. When time was called, the result was a goal each.

Stalvbridge refused to play extra time, conse- quently Earlestown had to pay them a visit on Tuesday afternoon to replay the tie. They were without Leigh at full back, and Limond, but they pressed Stalybridge considerably. The game was exceedingly rough and Earleetown were beaten by 3 goals to nil.

On Saturday they play St Helens Rees at St Helens in the EngHsu Cup competition when a tight game is expected.

Newton had a bul time of it at Wrexham on Saturday. They had no chance whatever with the Welshmen, who overplayed them to the extent of 9 goals to 1.

The tradesmen of Earlestown are in a fair way to get their fingers badly burnt. They have not publicly disclosed their hand yet, but it is becom- ing well known that they are organising their forces for a bout with he Co-operative Society, on the lins of the contest carried on at St Helen. It is agreed on all hands that they are very ill-advised. A combtit with the Earlestown society is sure to end disastrously for the tradesmen, in more ways than one, consequently if they wish to keep their feet they hal better let well alone. Earlestown is not St Helens, a tact these misguided tradesmen will discover to their sorrow.

EDUCATION.

Mr. E. J. Guest and the Member for Eddisbury Division.

Interesting Correspondence on the Govern- ment Bill. (To the Editor of the ".Ertneiner.") _

Sire—l shall esteem it a favour if you will kindly find a place in the next issue of your widely read paper for the enclosed copy of correspondence passed between the bon member for the Eddisbury division of Cheshire and myself. The subject of the Elucation Bill may to some appear rather stale ; but it is nevertholess necessary to keep it ' well before the public that out of the controversy there may yet emerge an issue that will put this country on right lines on ;his vexed question.—Srours faithfully,

E. J. GUEST.

To the Honourable Henry James Tollemacbe, Member of Parliament for the Eddisbury Division of Cheshire. Dear Sir,—At the Quarterly meeting of the officials of five Wesleyan Churches comprising the Frodsham Circuit it was unanimously resold in a wet attended gathering held 17th September, 1902: " That we should respectfully state our objections to the Education Bill, the discussion of which is shortly to be resumed in the House of Commons. Our objection to this Bill coming ' into force are briefly, that in our opinion 1 It will tend to postpone indefinitely the introduc- tion in this country of a truly national system of educa- tion so greatly needed. . . . . . .

2 'Cis franned in the interests cf the State Churth and will work to the infringement,of the rights of the Free Churches.

3. Whilst demanling the peyment of money out of the pockets cf the people, it strikes a blow at the con- stitutional right of the people who pay to control the expenditura of public money. . .

_ 4. N 4 mandate from the Constituencies has been given to sanction the forcing of this Bill through Parlia- ment ; on the contrary there are very many clear indications that a very strong, wide-spread, and locreas;.ng dislike to the Bill is being manifested through- out the country. In the interest of Education, of religious Equality, of the rights of the payers of rates and taxes, and of the Constituencies, we respectfully submit that it is most desirable that this Bill should be withdrawn. As our representative we earnestly prLy that you will use the power vested in you to oppose the Bill. Far better that an opportune moment should be awaited when this question of secular education can be permanently settled without provoking religious bitter ness.—We are, dear sir, yours respectfully, REV. E. GELDER, Minister, E. .1. GUEST, Circuit Steward, 1 S. WOOD WARD, Secretary. Dorfold, Naotwich, September Nth, 1902. Dear Sir,—l have received your letter and ri-quest that I will oppose the Education Bill of the Gowrnment. 1 do not think that tde gloomy forebodings you indulge in as to the wcnking of the Bill, if it brcpmes law, are really justified. Indeed I hope that, though doubtless suscep- tible of amendment, it may be given a trial by the country, in which case most of the difficulties anticipa- ted in your lel ter will, in my opinion, disappear in prac- tice.—Yours faithfully, _ HENRY I. TOLLEMACHE. _ .

Mr. E. J. Guest. Oak Mount, Helsbv, Warrington. 26th September, 1902. H. J. Tollemache. Esq., M.P. . .

Dear Sir,—l am much Obliged by your favour of the 24th instant, acknowledging the receipt of a resolution against the Education Bill which was passed at the Quarterly Meeting of the Frodsham Circuit oa the 17th September. As it seems to me that you do not realize the strength and importance of the opposition to this Bill I shall publish this correspondence in the Chester and Warrington papers. The Free Churches of this country werelnever stronger than they are to-day, and represent more ithan one-half of the population taking interest in Church matters. Almost unanimously they feel that , this Bill strikes at their position, and will do them a serious injustice. The Wesleyan Methodist Church is not a political church, and embraces members of various shades of opinion in political matters. But, whilst this is well known, you may have noticed that in all the District Syneds, and in the Annual Conference recently held in Manchester, this Education 811 has been condemned by overwhelming majorities. In the other Free Churches the opposition to this Bull is, if possible, greater. The great leaders in this opposition are men of wide culture, saintly lives, and broad sympathies who take a wide outlook en this and other questions. They are of opinion that this Bill will stultify education, and lead to great bitterness of feeling, resulting from a sense of injustice. Apart from the Churches there is a strong opposition to the Bill as e vinced in Bury, Leeds and Seven Oaks, and 1 fsel sure that it is not politic) on the part of the present Govern- ment to force their Bill through Parliament. If it should pass, tha feeling is so strong against it, that it will be unworkable. I;am yours respectfully. E. J. GUEST. Dorf old, September 29th 1902. _ .

Dear Sir,— I have no objection to your publishing this c)rrespondence, though it might have been more considerate had you intimated your intention of doing so in your first letter. I do not think that I underestimate the extent or vigour of the opposition t) the Education Bill, but I do not believe that the whole torrent of hos- tility rises from one source. There is the opposition of such men as you speak of, those who honestly think that the Bill is in many ways a bad and even dangerous measure, but who even in their opposition have the cause of education et heart. This is a class of opposi- tion which one values highly and which deserves not only the most careful attention, hut the most generous and sympathetic consideration. There is also the opposition which proceeds from mere Sectarian rancour and there is the opposition which (irrespective of the merits of the question), gladly seizes on any weapon, as for instance the export duty on coal, or the registration duty on corn, by which the Government may be weakened or defeated. These forms of opposition have of course to be taken into account, but do not deserve the same recognition as the former case.

I hope that-even yet the Bill may emerge from the committee stage in a form which may receive ' fairly general acceptance from reasonably minded men ; I hope in particular that provision will be made in it for enabling every religious body to give religious instruction to their children in all elementary schools, and personally speaking, I should wish, if possible to see increased representation given to the rating authority,especially where,tbe Denominational School is the only one available in any district. You tell me that the leaders of this movement against the Bill are men of " saintly lives and broad sympathies " and I fully accept your statement ; you and they will am sure, gladly credit the supporters of the Bill with similar virtues, but I cannot help thinking that gentle- men on both sides wont] more effectively demonstrate their claim to the possession of such desirable qualities were they to endeavour to come to some satisfactory compromise instead of condemning root and branch every suggeation put forward by those opposed to them and even in some .cases,declaring their intention ofrefus in to pay their Isgal obligations,in event of their wishes bung disregarded,—l remain dear sir, yours faithfully, HENRY J TOLLEMACHE. E. J. GUEST, Esq. . . .

Your letter of the 29th inst is now before me and I take it to be your final answer. I beg to thank you for the courtesy you have shown in this correspondence; but you will quite understand that your views on this vexed question will not satisfy the Free Churches of this country. In reg- rd to the legitimacy of refusing to pay rates of this Bill should neeoine law, whilst it cannot be denied that the imposition of the rate will be legal there is something higher than the law of one's country. Those who firmly believe that the rate for education imposed by this tfill,should it come into force, will be the revival of the old church rate in a veiled form, feel that they will be justified in offering the same resistance 199 was off ared by our fathers In former days to the church rate.—l am yours respectfully E. J. GUEST.

educational matters, give complete public contra, He asked them to give him credit for the fact that as a practical business man he had taken some active interest in his own town in the question before them. He realized its vast importance,and he wished to impress his own views and to ask them to take them for what they were worth." The question of Technical Instruction," he continued, " has increasingly occupied the attention of our foremost scientiers,educationalists,commercial Meth and oven statesmen for many years. Until now (although our methods for its inculcation are tar from perfect) its absolute necessity bas become so far eenerally recog- nized that the term of designation of ' Technical School' has become almost a household word. In order that we may better appreciate the National attitude on this important question to-day, it may herhaps be advisable to take a slightly restrospective view of our past commercial history. Teree or four generations ago by the wonderful discovery of the method of the appli- cation of stein power to purposes of locomotion and to the provision of motive power for manufacturieg pur- poses, combined with the rapid development of the enerrnous mineral wealth of the United Kingdom, aided by the adoption of a sound and wise fiscal policy, this country earned, and for a Tong period of years retained the right to the title of ' The Workshop of the World.' Daring this era, in comparison with the other European nations, who were largely occupied with internal dis- sensions, the prosperity of Engleud grew by leaps and bounds.' In the last decade or two, however, a great deal has happened. America has recovered from the deadly struggle which at the time threatened to throttle all possibilities of her progress for an unlimited perioi. Se rapid has been iher recovery, so illumitable are her natural resources so ntense is her commercial vitality, that she is becoming, if she has not already become, our most formidable rival in the open markets of the world. Germany, after the consolidation of her Empire, resultant upon the statesmenshio of Bismarck, and the military genius of Moltke, and enriched by the millions paid to her by trance after the great war, by the application of the teachings of scientific research to practical manufacture, and most materially assisted by an educational system which is thorough ion character, and absolutely untram- melled by sectarian differences, has begotten and fostered her trade and commerce to such an extent that we find her manufactures in every country and her Mips in almost every port. To a business man a very interesting feature of a continental trip, especially in Germany, will be found in examination of the commercial museums established in every town, and which are replete with specimens of the products of the arts and crafts of the surrounding district. So excellent is the classification of the exhibits that a visit of a few hours to such a museum will give one more information with the least possible effort, than can b 3 obtained in any but a few towns in this country, after days of labour. Although this has been somewhat of a digression I think you will pardon me for making it, because I want to show you that while we started in the commercial race with the enormous advantages to which I referred at the beginning of my remarks, we have, during the past 20 years, comparatively speaking, lost ground, and the reason is not far to seek. During the long peried of trade inflation already mentioned, a spirit of more or les justifiable pride and confidence was engendered in our business men, which became commercially speaking, a national characteris- tic, and with it all was combined an intense in- difference to change, and opposition to the adoption of anything new in manufacture. With comparatively few exceptims until a few years ago, because economy in maeufacture—(and I use the term economy in its widest application)—was not absolutely necessary from a prefit-making point of view,scientific eoonomiza- tion of fuel was unknown. What is the condition of things to-day ? So rapidly and enormously has the com- petition begotten of Arni ries and Continental induatry and eraterprize become, that we are absolu!ely compelled from motives o? self-preservation, to inquire into any and every possible difference between foreign methods and ours, in order to discover where our disability is to be found. This leads me to say that it is now admitted on all hand that the Continental youth are better equipped for their life's work than ours, owing to their superior educational advantales. In this eennection it is interesting to note the opinions of eminent scientists as expressed by them quite recently. Speaking at the Belfast meeting of the British Association a few weeks ago Profeseer Dewar, president, said : " The really ap- palling thing was not that the Germans have seized this or the other industry, or even that they may have seized upon a dozen industries, but that the German population has reached a point of general training, and specialised equipment,which will take us two genera- tions of hard and intelligently directed educational work to attain." These remarks were emphasised by other men of considerable eminence, and are incontro- vertible . This inequality as at present existing,will not I hope last much louger,and the period of its duration will be in proportion to the interest, or the lack of it !'s this important question of technioal education, which 's displayed both by parents and young people. °se ttlng is absolutely certain, and that is, that unless the risirig generation are enabled to realize that their own vidual successl depends largely upon their acquisition of every auxiliary qualification for the particular trade or pursuit which they have chosen to follow, seeing that they are units in the ageragation of the men and women to whom the destinies of our Empire would be committed in a few years from now, a want of a thorough appreciation cf their duty in this respect will certainly result in commercial disaster. To the parents who are here to-night I would most earnestly appeal that they should make what- ever sacrifices may be necessary—and after all they cannot be very great—to enable their song and daughters to avail themselves of any and every op- portunity afforded them for self improvement, bearing in mind that each additional talent so acquired not only carries with it a money value, but gives its possessor a bigger and better view of life and promotes better citizenship. (Applause.) In addressing my concluding remarks to the young people present may I say that your responsibilities to yourselves, to your parente and to your country, are as great as your privileges. You have advantages which a past generation never dreamed of, but at the same time it is only fair to say that you will need them all. • Depend upon it the youth who is wildly ambitious, who by a judicious use of his oppor- tunities acquires not only all that may be necessary to enable him to become a specialist in a miner way in his own particular avocation, but in addition is neither too idle nor apathetic to acquire knowledge of a more general character, and which at the moment may seem of little importance from the point of view of its future usefulness, will sooner or later achieve that success which diligence alone merits and opportunity invariably affords," (Applause.) Alderman Roberts then distributed the awards.

Technical Education. Speech by Alderman Roberts. The Educational Advantages of the Continental Youth.

On Monday evening Alderman Roberts distributed prizes gained during the last session by the s'udents at the Lymm Technicil Classes, and delivered an interest- ing speech on the subject of Technical education. In the course of his remarks he congratulated the Chairman (Mr C Kidson) and the committee upon the excellent attendance at tho meeting, and said be wished they eould in proportion to their population in Warrington get as big a meeting ac that upon that very interesting and important subject. LOCAL REVISION COURTS. °Rommel,. A Boer Claims a Vote.—lnteresting Point. Mr Clement E Scott, revising barrister, attended at the Large Room, on Tuesday,for the purpose of revising the voters' lists for the parish of Grappenhall, and the polling district of Warrington, with the Parliamentary division of Knutsford. The proceedings were of a routine character. Those present were—Mr Woodcock, assistant overseer for Gtappenhall ; Mr W H Woodcock, assietant overseer for Warrington ; and Mr Joseph Smith, for Knutsford. Mr Dean, of Knutsford, repre- sented the Conservative party and Mr Joseph Smith wai in charge of the Liberal interests. Mr Dean applied for a vote on bettalt of Samuel Fredeaux, of Grappenhall. Mr Smith objected on the ground that the claimant was a Boer and not a naturalised British subject. He wag born in South Africa forty years ago, but for some years had been living in England and had not taken out his naturalisation papers. -- Mr Dean contended that claimant was naturalised as ywe aoors..tion of the Transvaal all Boers became British subjects. Naturalisation papers were not neces- sary. Mr Smith saii the claimant was born outside British dominions and bad not taken out naturalisation papers. Continuing he admitted that claimant would have been a British subject had he remained in South Africa, but as he had been living in England for some years the annexation of the Transvaal made no difference to him. The Barrister asked Mr Smith if, in his opinion, the claimant would have become a naturalized subject if he had been living in South Africa now. Mr Smith replied in the affirmative. RiThe Barrister gelled if the fact of his living in England ha l to prevent him becoming naturalized by the annexa- tion of the Transvaal. Mr Smith contended that it bad. The Barrister said it was A very curious point. If claimant had been living in South ' Africa he would undoubtedly have become a British subject at oast, and he (the Barrister) did not sea how the mere haat of him living is England was to preclude him from becoming a British subject. Mr Smith did not agree with the Barrister on that paint. The Barrister said it was absurd to say that because claimant was not living in South Africa he did not become a naturalized subject. All thr Boers were now British subjects. Mr Dean said the alaimant bad been living in this country eleven years. The war was over the question of the franchise and he thought it wou!d be a shame if they did not give claimant his vote on the ground that he had not become naturalized. Mr Smith said the claim had nothing to do with the war. The Barrister said the point was not at all clear. If Claimant had been living in South Africa he would undoubtedly have become a British fubject. but still he would not have had a vote because the Boers had not yet been granted the franchise. The question Was as to whether during the period he bad lived in England claimant had lived as a British subject. He (the Barris- ter) Was not in a position to decide the point then, and he would give his decision the following day at Dares- bury. At the Daresbury Revision Court, on Wednesday, the Barrister allowed the claim. He said the Transvaal was now part of the Britieh Empire, and British subjects in Eegland fulfilling the necessary oonditions were entitled to a Vote.

THE REAPERS. The risen Sun, with glory crowncd, Resumes his heavenly race ; No clouds conceal his wondrous round, No shadows dim his face. Thus breaks the morn, all calm and bright, O'er hills and valleys fair : Unfolding in its golden light, The world of beauty there. On such a morn we love to rise, And wonder forth at will ; To feast our hearts, and feast our eyes, On nature lovely still. And then we see the rural bands, Once mere to labour born ; Go forth, with willing hearts and hands, To reap the yellow corn. Then 0, bow sweetly hums the bee, To gladden their employ ; And singing birds on every tree? With then would share their 30y: Intent on useful labour too, His web the spider weaves ; All white and lovely as the dew, That trembles on the leaves. God bless the reapers in their toil, Their brawny arms sust.iin ; That they may gather in tbe spoil, And fill our barns again. :Mr winter time is drawing nigh, 'Twill soon be at our door; When bleak and bare the fields will lie And yield their fruit no more. J. H. W. CLINDINNING. September. 1902.
